#3981. What a Fictional Robot Can Teach Us About Care

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Become a subscribing member today. Help us continue to bring “the science of a meaningful life” to you and to millions around the globe.

Greater Good Science Center • Magazine • In Action • In Education

This essay contains mild spoilers for the novel Klara and the Sun.

The baby, my baby, was to be named Josie. Josie Joy.

But when she was born, she was decidedly not a Josie. The name was too soft, too ethereal for such a sentient, wide-awake, bird-like baby. I knew that I had to find a new name. Who are you? I whispered into the swirls of her astoundingly warm, circulated, and singularly perfect ear. I couldn’t wait to get to know her.

In another story, a girl named Josie is dying. In Kazuo Ishiguro’s 2021 novel Klara and the Sun, Josie has an unknown illness and a prognosis that haunts the pages. Her life exists in the shadow of her sister, Sal, who has already died of the same unarticulated illness, likely caused by an elective genetic alteration procedure known as “lifting.”

In Josie’s world, being lifted is the new social currency; those who are lifted are expected to have greater access to both intellectual and economic success. The procedure does not, however, come without risk. When we meet Josie’s mother, she is tasked with the impossible: coping with the impending death of her only remaining child. A death likely resulting from a choice she made as a parent. The ultimate consequence.

Klara is a robot purchased by Josie’s mom to be her companion, in part (it is implied) to assuage the guilt she feels. I first read Ishiguro’s Klara and the Sun shortly after its debut. Despite being disturbed by some aspects of the dystopian world depicted within, my memory of the novel was poignant. I recalled Klara with particular affection.

Now, the distance between Ishiguro’s dystopian world and our current reality is rapidly collapsing. With the much anticipated film adaptation set to be released this Friday, October 23, and the sudden ubiquity of artificial intelligence in our day-to-day lives, Klara and the Sun is now repositioned as a mirror. If we look closely enough, we might discern what remains uniquely human during this moment of profound technological and social change.

We are introduced to Klara, the story’s narrator, when Josie’s mom takes her to find an artificial friend, or AF for short. In the novel, AFs confer social standing, but also offer practical assistance—tutoring, companionship, monitoring an ailing child—not unlike the benefits purported by artificial intelligence companies today.

Klara has been patiently waiting, alongside several other AFs, to be chosen. Josie’s mother is the final decider. In a foreboding scene, Josie’s mother commands Klara to reenact Josie’s gait—a test, of sorts—before agreeing to purchase her. It’s the precision of Klara’s mimicry that convinces “the Mother” (as she’s called in the novel) that Klara is the right model for Josie.

All of this, we learn through Klara. Ishiguro’s choice to position the reader within Klara’s perspective allows us to bypass the quagmire question of whether or not Klara is conscious and instead project our own consciousness onto her. In doing so, we view the human world through her sometimes malfunctioning robot eyes, as she nonjudgmentally conveys the complexities of the very flawed and sometimes desperate human behaviors she seeks to understand. In one particularly moving scene, Klara witnesses the vulnerability of caring for another human being:

I saw before me, on the near edge of the bed, a large shifting shape, made additionally complex by the patches of blackness and moonlight moving over its surface. I realized the shape was the Mother and Josie embracing. . . . As well as their limbs, their hair had become mingled, and then their shape began gently to rock, in a way not unlike when their goodbyes became extended.

“Don’t want to die, Mom. I don’t want that.”

“It’s okay. Okay.” The Mother’s voice was soft, at just the same level mine had been.

Prior to this moment, Klara—steadfast and ever-available—had offered her presence to comfort Josie. Yet, despite their far more complicated and at times strained relationship, it is the Mother that Josie calls for.

When I first became a parent, I confided to a friend that when my not-Josie was born, I felt like a new sun had burst into my solar system, and I was now orbiting around her. I couldn’t quite articulate the “before,” in my metaphor. I didn’t feel like I was previously my own sun, nor did I feel like I was a planet untethered to others. But there was something distinctly different when she arrived, a whole new consciousness, a space and force and awareness created within me. Her pull was intoxicating, undeniable, sometimes terrifying, and my awareness and attention seemed to expand overnight to now include not only my own experiences, but also to track, perceive, anticipate, and accommodate her own.

If Klara had turned to psychological theory and research about the complexity of parent-child bonds, she would have found a great deal of it. Consider Winnicott’s “good-enough mother” or research indicating that parents and infants are rarely, if ever, perfectly attuned, and the growing body of evidence suggesting that the navigation of the ruptures and repairs themselves reinforce attachment. It has become increasingly clear through evolving applications of experiments such as Edward Tronick’s now famous “still-face” experiment that the imperfect dance between caregiver and child lays the groundwork for resilience and emotional regulation.

In The Philosophical Baby, developmental psychologist and philosopher Alison Gopnik says, “It’s not so much that we care for children because we love them, as that we love them because we care for them.”

Beyond Gopnik’s own research, there is an abundance of data to back this up. Studies I have long been familiar with, such as those demonstrating that in same-sex, non-carrying male couples, fathers who act as primary parents show changes in similar brain regions to women who carried their pregnancies and go on to parent their children. Yet, there was something profound about the simplicity of Gopnik’s statement, about love being something we cultivate within ourselves and, more precisely, about care being the means through which we build love, not merely a necessary byproduct.

In a separate interview, Gopnik jokes that spending time with a three-year-old gives you a small taste of sainthood. Not in the sense that the demands are so extreme that you inherently become a saint (though there is a real argument to be made here for any parent who weathers the storm of a parking lot tantrum), but in the actual felt-sense of the divine, the experience of feeling so acutely and utterly in love with this being, so in awe of their singularity, their specialness, their openness to experience, their brilliance.

Gopnik continues on, teasing, about our tendency to claim the uniqueness of our three-year-old, in her case her grandchild, but gently affirms the truth beneath: that there is something numinous, universal, pure within all three-year-olds, a near-magical vitality that we sometimes make contact with when we intimately care for one. Something primal, or perhaps primary, is awoken when we re-experience the world through the eyes of a three-year-old.

My not-Josie is now three. In many ways she is more unknowable, separate, than she was when she was a newborn, so physically attached and dependent on me. Now, she has her own relationships—with teachers, classmates, family members—that are entirely her own. And yet, she occupies and orients so much of me, continually changing my experience of the world, my access to play, to awe, to unadulterated, intoxicating adoration.

This past summer, I was sitting by a lake, relaxed but hawk-eyed as I watched my not-Josie from the shore. I felt a familiar presence by my side, the barely-perceptible shadow in my peripheral vision of a small body sitting down, and soon thereafter, the unselfconscious weight of a small hand on my leg. I looked down to see a head covered in feathery blonde hair, not the dark, curly brown of my own child.

The wispy tufts belonged to another small child who had clearly mistaken me for her mother. In that brief moment before she looked up, before we both acknowledged our separateness, I felt overcome with the awareness—as though received through the touch of her hand on my leg—that her mother loved her in the way that I love my child, and that, despite how unbelievably special my not-Josie is, my love for her is not. It is precious, but also universal.

Intellectually, of course, this is not a profound realization. Emotionally touching into it is, on the other hand, equally as brutal as it is beautiful. Next to this small child, healthy and safe, I felt her preciousness, but also that of all the children flooding my news feed every day who are not healthy and safe, and whose parents must live with this love and that devastation.

On the back cover, Klara and the Sun is described as a story about love, which it undeniably is. But I think it’s perhaps more fundamentally about care.

Care is arguably more tangible than love, but also more complex, friction-prone, messy. It’s the gaps, the mistakes, the harms, the repairs, the precarity, the forgiving, that make care so deeply painful and so deeply nourishing. Nourishing enough to sustain the vulnerability of love.

It is care that drives Josie’s mother to have her “lifted” in an age-old attempt of desperation to give her child what she perceives as the best chance at a full life in a rapidly reorganizing world. It is care that sends the Mother down the path of trying to find a way to have Klara “continue” Josie’s life, even though she has already tried and failed to recreate her first child’s consciousness in an artificial intelligence through the work of an artist and scientist named Capaldi.

It is care that allows the Mother—despite her cold exterior—to fearlessly comfort her child as she confronts the possibility of her own death, enveloping her, rocking her, soothing her through the unimaginable. It is the Mother’s human care, not her cowardice, that makes her love true.

There is so much in Klara and the Sun that is undeniably relevant in this particular social, cultural, and technological moment, so many ways in which Ishiguro’s Josie and my not-Josie’s world are suddenly uncomfortably proximate, but it is what Klara herself affirms about humanity that is most resonant.

At the end of the novel, Klara reflects on what she’s learned. She did not have to become a Josie 2.0, for Josie survived. Klara is glad for this, of course, believing that it was her bargain with the Sun (which she sees as a kind of god) that saved Josie. Sitting discarded in a junk yard, Klara is increasingly convinced that the desperate plan to turn Josie into a digital consciousness wouldn’t have worked:

Mr. Capaldi believed there was nothing special inside Josie that couldn’t be continued. He told the Mother he’d searched and searched and found nothing like that. But I believe now he was searching in the wrong place. There was something very special, but it wasn’t inside Josie. It was inside those who loved her. That’s why I think now Mr. Capaldi was wrong and I wouldn’t have succeeded.

It is what happens within us and between us when we care for another being that differentiates us as humans. In other words, it’s not so much what’s there but what’s not there—the space between the person in front of us and how we perceive, imperfectly care for, and ultimately love them—that makes us human.

Through Klara’s eyes, I was able to see more clearly what connects the Mother and her Josie to me and my not-Josie. It is the very fact that we can so wholeheartedly love another being, without being them, having them, solving them, knowing every bit of them. It is the fact that still, we carry them in our hearts, and somehow we are not heavier for it.

Liza Ruggiero, M.A. , ACC, is a leadership coach, facilitator, educator, and writer whose work draws on psychology, mindfulness, and emotional intelligence to support personal and professional growth. She is the founder of Heartwork Leadership Practice, where she helps people navigating transitions, such as emerging leaders, new parents, and career changers, develop the self-awareness and self-regard to lead from a place of purpose.

#3971. Ten TV Series That Highlight the Best in Humanity: 2026

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Become a subscribing member today. Help us continue to bring “the science of a meaningful life” to you and to millions around the globe.

Greater Good Science Center • Magazine • In Action • In Education

Every year, we invite staff and regular contributors to nominate TV series for Greater Goodies, the awards we give to stories that exemplify or illustrate kindness, community, empathy, and more keys to well-being. Here’s what we came up with this year: The Boroughs, Death by Lightning, Envidiosa, The Hawk, Heated Rivalry, A Knight of the Seven Kingdoms, Lucky, Murderbot, Pluribus, and Shrinking.

Name the last show you’ve seen that features a hero over the age of 60. Drawing a blank? It’s not your fault—the media’s modus operandi is to equate heroism with youthfulness. Netflix’s The Boroughs flips this stereotype on its head.

With a star-studded lead cast, all 70 or older, we follow Sam Cooper’s (Alfred Molina) experience as he reluctantly moves into a senior living community in New Mexico after the death of his wife. But a series of strange events suggests that this is no ordinary retirement community.

As Sam navigates grief, new friendships, and a new identity, he joins forces with his neighbors to solve and resolve the community mystery. Overcoming obstacles like age discrimination and self-doubts, the group peels back the layers of the strange occurrences. At the root of the mystery is a societal fear of aging.

The show highlights what researcher Margie Lachman calls “crystallized intelligence”: the culmination of life experience, memory, and know-how. In The Boroughs, that turns out to be just as heroic as super human strength or the ability to fly. — Shanna B. Tiayon

History has already told us how Death by Lightning ends: Charles Guiteau will assassinate President James Garfield in 1881. But why? That’s one of the questions explored by the series, and it finds motivation in a sense of purpose–how purpose forms and how people come to cross-purposes.

Garfield (Michael Shannon) is principled and drawn reluctantly into a presidential contest he entered to nominate someone else. Guiteau (Matthew Macfadyen), meanwhile, is disturbingly sympathetic: charming, articulate, and desperate to do something meaningful with his life. Despite failed ventures, including a stint at the Oneida free-love commune, he remains convinced greatness awaits him. Believing his campaigning secured Garfield’s victory, he expects a government position to confirm his worth.

As their stories converge, Garfield finds purpose in his presidency: fighting corruption while supporting education and equal opportunity. Guiteau pursues a reward he feels he deserves, an entitlement that leads to tragedy.

In these ways, Garfield and Guiteau offer sharply contrasting answers to what makes a life meaningful, and watching their stories unfold side by side, we see how differently the desire to matter can play out.

Who you really ought to be watching closely is Garfield’s Vice President, Chester A. Arthur (Nick Offerman). A comfortable beneficiary of the political machine, he becomes an unlikely source of hope as the story goes on. Offerman makes him funny, flawed, and increasingly affecting as the demands of office begin to collide with old loyalties.

In a political landscape that can leave us cynical–in both the 19th century and the 21st–the series makes room for something more hopeful: the possibility that even an unlikely person can rise to the occasion, if they know why they need to rise. – Aurelia Santos

When almost-40-year-old Vicky (Griselda Siciliani) breaks up with her long-term boyfriend, she is driven to embark upon a journey of self-discovery.

At first, Vicky is just looking for a replacement for her boyfriend—but the problem is that her compulsive comparisons with other women sabotage attempts to connect with other people. (Indeed, the name of this Argentinian show, Envidiosa, means “envious.”) Vicky is self-absorbed and yet at the same time she doesn’t have a strong sense of her own self.

We see this vividly in her hilarious sessions with her therapist. When, for example, she goes on an envious rant about a friend’s milestone, she is forced to confront the quiet, uncomfortable realization that she never once asked how her friend actually felt. In short, Vicky is not a polished role model—and over the course of four seasons, we see her empathy grow through the painful process of unlearning self-centeredness.

Personally, I see Envidiosa as an opportunity to question how we show up for others’ joys and struggles, and I take it as an invitation to reflect: How can I empathize with the ups and downs of those around me? Envidiosa is a funny and at times embarrassingly relatable story about trying to become a more empathic human being and friend. — Criss Cuervo

In The Hawk, washed-up professional golfer Lonnie “The Hawk” Hawkins (Will Ferrell) has made a miracle comeback into the PGA tour—this time around, facing a tough competitor in his son, Lance (Jimmy Tatro).

It’s a screwball comedy, full of dirty jokes and a rapid-fire rapport between Lonnie and his not-quite-ex-wife, Stacy (Molly Shannon). A makeshift-caddy he “picked up at Walmart” (while chatting up the parking lot, from his camper van) adds to the fun, serving up an unlikely duo that seem happy just to be alive.

Even when he’s pretty down and out, Lonnie is looking on the bright side of things. Everything is done with gusto—he chats up the golf ball before each swing, he devours plates of Buffalo Wild Wings, he plays mini golf with his son and makes it a full-on competition.

“Son, never play golf angry,” he tells Lance. For Lonnie, life is all about golf—and golf is all about fun. Why spend all your time on something if you aren’t doing it for the joy of it?

I especially love Lonnie’s adoration for his estranged wife, a firecracker in her own right. In episode four, when the Hawk crashes his son’s engagement party–yes, you read that right–and then attempts a getaway with his caddie in the van, Stacy hurls the cake at them, along with a string of expletives.

It doesn’t even faze him. “You gotta love the passion!” Lonnie gushes. “That is one hot tamale!” — Hope Reese

Heated Rivalry follows stick-crossed lovers Shane and Ilya as they figure out how to be intimate within the highly competitive, homophobic environment of professional hockey.

At first, Shane and Ilya swap fluids more readily than emotions. But over the course of six episodes, the two learn to love across gaps in culture, language, and experience. In one pivotal scene, Shane invites Ilya to speak vulnerably about family dynamics in Ilya’s native Russian—despite not understanding a word of it.

That works because Shane does not focus attention on himself. Instead, he stays present on the phone, serving as a witness to Ilya’s visceral emotions. Their connection lies in felt care, in hearing the texture of another’s heartbreak.

In similar fashion, Ilya grounds Shane in stressful encounters. He supports Shane through panic without wallowing in his lover’s fear and entices Shane to relish pleasures outside of straight standards. As a couple, they help each other explore complex parts of themselves. We see a similar process unfold in another couple in the show, as they learn to respect each other’s different needs.

People might tune into Heated Rivalry for locker room towel drops and HBO’s glossy cinematography. They should stay to see a couple learn to love each other’s differences. The show exemplifies how bridging differences, at its best, means supporting others to more fully encounter themselves, and how in the process our relationships can grow.

With plenty of additional homoerotic stick-and-ball games to pull on from the novels of Rachel Reid, the next season of Heated Rivalry is expected to air on HBO Max and Canada’s Crave in Spring 2027. — Luke Brown

The monstrously successful HBO series Game of Thrones famously tossed out a lot of good-and-evil fantasy tropes to tell a morally complicated story of how power corrupts even the best people. A prequel called House of the Dragon shares that worldview.

That’s why a new prequel called A Knight of the Seven Kingdoms came as something of a surprise to fans. Unlike the other two series, this is not a story about a struggle for power. Instead, it focuses on a rather naive, not-too-bright “hedge knight” named Duncan (Peter Claffey) seeking to be hired by whatever lord will take him. Though Duncan is taller and stronger than most, he’s touchingly childlike as he tries to find his place in the merry old kingdoms of Westeros.

The first two episodes are funny and charming, as Duncan acquires a squire in the form of a little bald boy going by the name Egg (Dexter Sol Ansell), and fumblingly tries to enter a jousting tournament. And then Duncan finds himself on a collision course with the great and powerful of Westeros. When violence finally comes, there’s nothing casual about it. We feel the force of every blow, the tragedy of every death, and the erosion of Duncan’s innocence.

I’m giving A Knight of the Seven Kingdoms the award for integrity, but I can’t tell you why without spoiling the ending. Let’s just say that the series turns out to be the story of how one simple young man discovers what is most important to him—and finds the courage to stick to that path even in the face of isolation, temptations, and threats. — Jeremy Adam Smith

How do you exit a life you were born into?

That’s the question behind Lucky, starring Anya Taylor-Joy. Lucky was raised by her con-artist father and taught the “family business” from a young age—but when we meet her, she navigating internal conflict with the life she’s been told to live and the life she wants for herself.

Her desire to quit is complicated by her marriage to Cary, the son of a ruthless mob leader, who was also socialized into a life of crime. The couple plan one final con as their exit strategy, but it goes terribly wrong. Lucky sets out to resolve the botched plan, reconcile her marriage, and protect both her life and her father’s.

Her intelligence is put to the test as she puts the pieces together, connects the dots, and outsmarts her rivals. In the process she discovers her own virtue—and that the intelligence which made her a successful con artist can also set her free.

Through the course of the series, she realizes that she does not have to pay for the sins of her father. Having never been given the opportunity for self-determination, she makes peace in the best way she knows how and chooses a new path. — Shanna B. Tiayon

The Murderbot Diaries are a series of science-fiction novellas by Martha Wells, about a genderless, robotic soldier who manages to achieve free will. Since discovery will result in deactivation and since it doesn’t like humans very much anyway, Murderbot conceals its freedom of mind and consumes a lot of human-made entertainment inside its own head, even as it externally follows every order given to it by the evil corporation that owns it. Murderbot even has a favorite TV show: the Star-Trek-like Rise and Fall of Sanctuary Moon.

As a result of this isolation, the novellas are built almost entirely around its memorable inner monologues, with a voice that is by turns snarky, vulnerable, cold-blooded, disgusted, and puzzled. One of the things that makes these books distinctive is that Murderbot is not Pinocchio—it has no wish to become a “real boy.” It doesn’t want a gender, or the same feelings and drives as people. It just wants to be itself—and to have that difference respected.

While the Apple TV show based on the books is not quite that original or interesting, Murderbot retains its focus on finding the balance between belonging and difference. When we meet Murderbot (Alexander Skarsgård), he’s been hired to protect a group of polyamorous hippie scientists who hail from a utopian colony that exists independently of the corporations that rule the rest of the galaxy.

They are Murderbot’s first encounter with humans not shaped by dehumanizing hyper-capitalistic values, and they observe Murderbot in a way the corporate humans cannot. Over the course of the first season, they begin to realize that there is more to Murderbot than meets the eye, and they learn to see and appreciate Murderbot on its own terms. But what’s crucial is not that they perceive the humanity in Murderbot—it’s that Murderbot sees the humanity (and the goodness) in them.

In time, it becomes an accepted part of their society—without becoming more like them. Even so, the first season concludes on a tough-minded, melancholy note, as Murderbot itself can’t quite feel like it belongs. Will it someday find a place among these crunchy idealists? Stay tuned: The second season is coming in 2027. — Jeremy Adam Smith

Have you ever been among people but you didn’t feel part of the crowd? Like they were in on a joke that you just couldn’t get?

Welcome to Carol Sturka’s world. The science-fiction premise of Pluribus is that an alien virus transforms humanity into one big happy hive mind—everyone except Carol and 13 other people who seem to have immunity.

Even among those remaining individuals, though, Carol stands out as a malcontent. We sense that she’s been a fish out of water for her whole life.

Indeed, not every viewer will find Carol relatable or likable. When we first meet her, she’s at a bookstore event promoting her highly successful romantasy series. With her fans, she’s charming, funny, and kind. When she gets into a limo after a big sendoff by readers, the drivers asks if she’s famous.

“That depends,” Carol replies. “Are you a big fan of mindless crap?”

In short, Carol hates herself, her work, and her readers. And as we discover, her personality is snarky, uptight, stubborn, and uncompromising. When the virus hits, it’s an apocalyptic event, but Carol doesn’t change one bit—and her traits become both a blessing and a curse. The whole world wants to be kind to her, but Carol can’t accept kindness. That drives her to fight back. And that’s what ultimately makes her the hero of the story.

In the end, Pluribus is a very Greater Good thought experiment. It takes prosociality to its limits and finds that a total togetherness, empathy, and cooperation to the exclusion of selfishness, crankiness, and boundaries is a self-destructive dystopia, not a utopia. The show seems to argue that humanity needs difficult people like Carol to survive, and so invites us to appreciate what the Carols of the world have to offer. — Jeremy Adam Smith

In Shrinking, talking and listening are the name of the game. Jimmy (Jason Segel) is a widowed psychotherapist who helps patients navigate anxiety, OCD, PTSD, and other ailments as he himself is floundering.

But he’s not alone. His teenage daughter, overbearing-but-lovable neighbors, and coworker-friends (with Harrison Ford as the curmudgeonly head of the practice) are all there to help him when his life goes off the rails after his wife was killed by a drunk driver.

In its three-season run, the series is heavy-handed with its use of therapy-speak among the whole gang, whether between therapist-to-patient, therapist-to-daughter, or therapist-to-therapist. Characters “feel seen.” More times than I can count, they tell each other “I hear you.” We witness, in detail, exchanges inside the therapy office—and outside of it, in a rogue practice known as “Jimmying,” where therapy takes place at such venues as, for instance, the boxing ring.

In other words, there’s a lot of “deep listening”: the practice of being present, open, and empathic with another person, in a way that goes beyond just hearing and understanding words. In his grief, Jimmy himself is not always a perfect deep listener. He tends to talk more than a typical therapist, and frequently injects himself into the discussion. Shrinking isn’t about perfection—it’s about struggling to connect and growing in the process. – Hope Reese

Luke Brown is the Bridging Differences Facilitator of the Bridging Differences program at UC Berkeley’s Greater Good Science Center and a PhD candidate in higher education at Boston College. He creates conditions for people to more deeply encounter one another and embrace interdependency, restorative relation, and shared futures.

We learned a lot in producing our new documentary series, Connecting on Campus: Stories of Courage, Curiosity, and Empathy.

Undergraduate students surveyed their own generation—and found more optimism about bridging divides than in any other age group.

According to a new study, spoken conversations lead to more understanding, less conflict, and more openness to other opinions.

Across school campuses and communities, students and educators are discovering how listening, curiosity, and everyday conversations can open pathways across differences and help restore a sense of shared humanity.

What does it actually take to put people over politics? Republican and Democrat students discover ways to resist the pull to demean or villainize people with whom they strongly disagree.

#3962. How to Stay Informed But Not Overwhelmed by Election News

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Become a subscribing member today. Help us continue to bring “the science of a meaningful life” to you and to millions around the globe.

Greater Good Science Center • Magazine • In Action • In Education

You pick up your phone to check one thing—perhaps the weather, a text message, or the score of a game. A few minutes later, you have read three election headlines, watched a clip from a debate, skimmed dozens of comments, and somehow learned that democracy, the economy, your health care, and the future of the planet may all be in danger before breakfast.

If this sounds familiar, you are not alone.

Election season can make the news feel unusually personal. The issues on the ballot can affect our finances, health, schools, communities, and rights. At the same time, the way we now encounter political information—constantly, emotionally, and often in fragments—can leave us feeling overloaded rather than informed.

There is even a name for this experience: election stress. The Mayo Clinic describes it as the overwhelming anxiety some people feel during an election cycle. And surveys from the American Psychological Association suggest that elections have become a significant source of stress for many Americans. In the lead-up to the 2024 presidential election, for example, 72% of adults said they were worried that the results could lead to violence, and 56% said they feared the election could mark the end of democracy in the United States.

It is understandable to feel unsettled when an election seems to carry serious consequences. But anxiety is not always a problem to get rid of; in small doses, it can direct our attention toward what matters. It may motivate us to learn, vote, volunteer, have difficult conversations, or support people affected by political decisions.

The problem begins when information meant to help us engage instead keeps our nervous systems in a constant state of alarm.

Part of election stress comes from the uncertainty it elicits. We do not know who will win, what policies will change, or how the people around us will respond. Research suggests that uncertainty itself can be stressful because people naturally want to understand and predict the environment around them. When we feel unable to do that, we may feel less safe and less in control.

News can intensify that uncertainty. Negative news is often more attention-grabbing than positive news, and conflict, danger, and outrage make especially compelling headlines. That does not mean journalists are intentionally trying to harm us. Reporting on threats and problems is an essential part of journalism. But a steady diet of alarming information can shape how we feel about the world, especially when it leaves little room for context, solutions, or signs of human cooperation.

This can create a difficult cycle. When people feel anxious, they often seek out more information in an effort to regain a sense of control. Yet the more election news they encounter—particularly news marked by uncertainty, conflict, and emotional intensity—the more anxious they may feel. Then they check again.

In other words, we may turn to the news to calm ourselves down, only to leave feeling more activated than before.

Political stress is not new. But the way we receive political information has changed dramatically.

When John F. Kennedy and Richard Nixon debated on television in 1960, viewers watched in a far more contained media environment. There was no endless stream of clips, reactions, memes, push notifications, and commentary arriving at every hour of the day. People could discuss the debate afterward, but they were not also carrying the debate around in their pockets.

Today, election news can follow us almost everywhere: to the grocery store line, the waiting room, the car, the dinner table, and bed. Social media makes it easier for people to participate politically and amplify voices that were once excluded from public conversation. Those are meaningful benefits. But the same technology can make political information feel impossible to put down.

It also encourages us to consume news in small, emotionally potent pieces. A headline while brushing our teeth. A video clip while waiting for coffee. A dramatic post at a stoplight. These snippets can provoke strong feelings without giving us enough context to understand the story fully.

That matters because headlines often tell us what to feel before they tell us what happened. Pew research found that five out of eight Americans (63%) said they only read news headlines rather than full news articles, specifically when scrolling through social media. When people repeatedly take in emotionally charged fragments of news, they may feel a growing sense of threat while having less detailed information to guide their judgments.

Faced with this constant emotional demand, some people keep checking the news compulsively. Others decide they have had enough and stop paying attention altogether.

News avoidance can offer temporary relief. If every update makes you tense, angry, or helpless, closing the app may feel like self-protection. In fact, a report from the American Press Institute found that a third of Americans want to disengage from news because it feels stressful and overwhelming.

Tips for managing election stress while building a better democracy

But avoiding news altogether comes with costs, especially during an election year. It can make it harder to participate in democratic life, understand issues that affect our communities, or distinguish reliable reporting from misinformation passed along by friends, influencers, or highly partisan accounts.

The goal, then, is not to consume more news or no news. It is to develop a healthier relationship with news: one that helps us stay informed without asking our nervous systems to be on call all day.

1. Choose a news source that includes solutions, not only problems. During stressful times, it can help to seek out reporting that does more than identify what is going wrong. Constructive journalism still covers serious problems, but it also provides context, examines solutions, and asks what people and communities can do next. This approach is not about pretending everything is fine. It is about giving readers a more complete picture: What is the problem? Who is affected? What is being tried? What can citizens realistically do? Solution-oriented reporting may be especially helpful during elections because it can replace helplessness with a more grounded sense of agency and hope for the future.

When you choose a news source, consider asking: Does this reporting leave me better informed? Does it help me understand the issue, or does it only make me feel alarmed? Does it explain possible solutions as well as the threat?

2. Balance distressing news with inspiring media. We do not need to force ourselves to be positive about difficult realities. But we can balance the emotional weight of election coverage with media that remind us of human goodness, beauty, courage, and connection.

Research suggests that stories of kindness, moral courage, and collective care can evoke feelings such as elevation, gratitude, and hope. These emotions can broaden our view of what is possible and help us feel more connected to other people. And new research from the University of Santa Barbara suggests that these emotions can even reduce stress.

This might mean watching a short video about neighbors helping one another after a disaster, reading a profile of local organizers working across differences, listening to music that helps you settle, or returning to a film or show that leaves you feeling more hopeful about people, or nostalgic about the past.

Think of this as building a more balanced media diet. Election news can be an important part of that diet, but it should not be the only thing we feed our minds. Just as eating one healthy meal a week does not transform our physical health, one inspiring story will not undo chronic stress. Over time, though, regularly choosing media that restore us, physically and mentally, can help cultivate resilience, hope, and perspective.

3. Create boundaries around when you check the news. Finally, consider treating news less like a snack and more like a meal.

Snacking on news all day can mean consuming a lot without ever feeling nourished. You absorb a headline here, an alert there, and a few comments in between—but never get enough context to make sense of what you are seeing. The result can be a lot of emotional calories and very little understanding.

Instead, choose one or two intentional times to engage with the news. You might read a full article over breakfast, listen to a trusted news program on your commute, or set aside 15 minutes at lunch to review the day’s major stories. If you are prone to checking headlines compulsively, digital wellness strategies such as app limits or notification settings can make those boundaries easier to keep.

It may also help to avoid making late-night news “snacking” part of your bedtime routine. By the end of the day, our emotional and cognitive resources are often already depleted. A stream of alarming headlines can quickly turn into doomscrolling: the repeated consumption of negative online content that leaves us more agitated and less able to sleep.

Giving news its own place in the day does not mean becoming indifferent. It means making room to take in information carefully, in context, and with enough emotional space to respond thoughtfully.

Election stress is real because elections matter. Feeling concerned about the future does not mean that something is wrong with you; it may mean you are paying attention.

But paying attention does not require constant exposure to distress. We can choose reporting that informs and empowers us, balance difficult coverage with media that reminds us of human possibility, and set boundaries that give our minds a chance to rest.

The aim is not emotional numbness. It is becoming the kind of informed citizen who can face difficult news, stay connected to what matters, and still have enough calm and hope left to act.

Sophie H. Janicke, Ph.D., is a positive media psychologist working as an associate professor at Chapman University, who investigates the role that new and traditional media play in promoting and affecting character development, self-transcendent emotions (such as elevation, gratitude, and awe), prosocial behavior, and happiness.

#3953. What’s Important to Us When Making Political Decisions?

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Become a subscribing member today. Help us continue to bring “the science of a meaningful life” to you and to millions around the globe.

Greater Good Science Center • Magazine • In Action • In Education

A core assumption in any democracy is that citizens with access to information will make reasoned decisions on political candidates and policy issues. But a new study by UC Berkeley social scientists finds that Americans usually don’t consider the full scope of difficult issues, instead basing their opinions on mental shortcuts that sidestep complexity.

The paper offers striking insight into the differences in cognitive reasoning patterns among Americans. While some people in the study did closely analyze a range of issues before coming to an opinion, others did a quick back-of-the-napkin tally of plusses and minuses, or even more commonly based their opinions on just one factor, the authors found.

“When people make complicated choices with many considerations—which political candidate to support, which immigrant they would rather admit to the country, which climate policy they prefer—we tend to assume they’re weighing all the factors,” said Berkeley political scientist Kirk Bansak. But, he added, “we find that most of them aren’t. Most commonly, people rely on a mental shortcut, effectively deciding based on the single factor they care about most and largely ignoring the rest.”

For example, when asked to choose between two fictional U.S. Senate candidates based on age, gender, race, education, military experience, and political experience, many of the study participants would make their decision based on just one of those factors.

That doesn’t mean that people are irrational, said coauthor Nidhi Banavar, a Berkeley cognitive scientist. “The kind of rationality that we define in the paper is quite different from what many humans actually do in real life,” she explained. “We have these amazing brains, but they’re not the infinite computational machines that would be necessary in order to do the kind of ‘rational’ we imagine in our paper.”

Still, the findings have clear implications for politics and political communication. For example, Banavar said, if a leader knows that public opinion on climate change is dominated by pocketbook concerns to the exclusion of other issues, then that single factor might weigh heavily in political advertising. Complex science issues, meanwhile, might be downplayed.

The paper, “Classifying Strategy Use in Multi-Attribute Subjective Choice: Application to Conjoint Experiments in Political Science,” was published recently in Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences. Bansak is an associate professor of political science at UC Berkeley; Banavar is a postdoctoral researcher in political science at UC Berkeley.

The foundational ideals of American democracy grew from the Enlightenment era some three centuries ago. Central to that framework was a belief that humans, being rational, are capable of governing themselves—and justified in demanding political power. A core concept has been the marketplace of ideas, a constant competition between political and policy goals, plans, and analysis that would inform citizens and help them to govern themselves.

While many eras in U.S. history feature episodes in which reason faltered, modern social scientists have detailed the ways in which cognitive bias, rational ignorance, and other behaviors short-circuit reasoning. Still, the hopeful assumption about human reasoning persists.

Social scientists have long studied human decision making at the aggregate level. Banavar and Bansak set out to build a new kind of cognitive model, a complex tool that can enable researchers to see an individual’s decision making mid-process, in detail.

The model doesn’t give them a view into the minds of their research subjects, but the researchers can study hundreds or thousands of decisions by an individual; from these patterns, they can perceive the values and reasoning processes that shape their decisions.

In all, nearly 3,800 people were involved in the study. Each participant sat at a computer screen, and the computer generated a random set of choices in three areas: which migrants they would rather admit to the United States, which Senate primary candidate they would rather vote for, and which climate policy package they would prefer the United States to adopt.

Consider the basic question comparing two hypothetical migrants to the U.S. Their profiles appeared side by side, each described by six factors: region of origin, education level, gender, age, English proficiency, and whether they were seeking economic opportunity or asylum. The research subject would have to choose which of the migrants they preferred to admit to the U.S.

The computer randomly presented 30 different sets of hypothetical migrants. If a participant consistently chose the younger migrant or the migrant with greater English skills, for example, the researchers could conclude that that was the most important factor for that participant.

The same decision framework was presented for comparing potential climate change policies and fictional U.S. Senate candidates.

The research did yield some insights about basic priorities: Participants preferred younger political candidates. They preferred climate change policy that minimized economic impact on their household. They preferred immigrants who were seeking asylum as opposed to seeking economic opportunity, and those who were below retirement age.

But that wasn’t the primary purpose of the study. Banavar and Bansak were less interested in what participants decided, and more interested in how they decided.

The results were striking: In 68% of the cases, study participants used shortcuts and did not deeply assess all six factors when choosing between hypothetical Senate candidates, migrants, or climate policies.

Nearly 54% showed a pattern of making choices dominated by one of the six factors. Another 14% took a different shortcut: Given six factors for each candidate or policy, they counted the total number of factors that they preferred for each, then chose the one containing the greater number.

How many used a comprehensive evaluation of all six factors—a rational assessment, without any shortcuts? Just under 30%.

Interestingly, Banavar said, a majority of participants did not consistently stay with the same strategy. Some might rely on just one factor in choosing a climate policy, while more fully weighing all the factors in deciding which migrant to admit.

“You can’t put people into buckets to say, ‘OK, this is someone who’s actually only going to care about only one thing,’” she said. “People are actually relating differently to each of these different domains and choosing to use different levels of effort, for want of a better term.”

To understand why people shift strategies will require further research, she said.

The study comes with some important caveats: If a participant favored female candidates, for example, or migrants who were seeking political asylum, and if both choices had those attributes, the participants had to consider other factors. That reduced, but did not eliminate, the pattern of making a choice based on one factor.

But when the researchers asked the participants to rate each profile before choosing one of them, the participants were more inclined to consider all factors in making their choices.

Further, the researchers allowed for only two attributes in their comparisons—male or female, for example, white or Black, or aged in their 50s or 70s. That’s not ideal, Banavar said, but to include multiple races, for example, is too complex for the cognitive model, and would dilute the patterns in the data. In the choice of Senate candidates, the study did not include the fictional candidates’ political parties. That question could provoke such strong, reflexive reactions that it would cloud the findings, she said.

The authors say their research has important implications not just for cognitive research, but for real-world, on-the-ground U.S. political processes.

Often, Bansak said, our understanding of public opinion is based on surveys that ask people to choose between complex options defined by many factors. The new research shows analysts that individuals often base their opinions on a single factor, and that can create a clearer picture of what people want in public policy, and why they want it.

“This bears directly on how we interpret the kind of public-opinion and preference data that routinely informs policy debates and news coverage,” he said.

And that understanding, Banavar said, can shape the policy process—and political communication. If leaders present issues in layers of complexity, “how much are people going to read or listen to that?” she asked. “What is the thing that’s really important to them, given a particular issue? That’s completely lost when you don’t think about the decision-making process.”

This article was originally published on Berkeley News. Read the original article.

Edward Lempinen is a writer and media relations specialist with the UC Berkeley Office of Communications and Public Affairs. He has extensive experience in science and science policy communication, including support for global high-level science diplomacy initiatives. He served as producer of the documentary film Science in Exile (2017), which focused on researchers from Syria, Yemen, and Iraq who have been displaced by recent wars and conflicts.

#3944. Can You Learn to Feel More Loved?

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Become a subscribing member today. Help us continue to bring “the science of a meaningful life” to you and to millions around the globe.

Greater Good Science Center • Magazine • In Action • In Education

Imagine you have a colleague who remembers that you had a difficult appointment and checks in on you. Or a friend who is just there to listen without telling you what you should have done differently. We don’t tend to think of these experiences as grand declarations of love, but they leave us with a certain lingering warmth: the feeling of being loved.

In our research, we coined the term felt love to focus on the receiver’s side of love. This emphasis shifts attention away from the expressions of love (i.e., loving signals). While expressions are actions we choose to take, felt love is an internal, subjective sensation that washes over us. However, how strongly it does so also depends on us: The same act of care may pass unnoticed or land as love, based on the receiver’s cognitive beliefs or state of mind. Many experiences may elicit felt love, and they occur not only in romantic settings, but when we share time with friends, receive supportive gestures from family, or even get unexpected help from strangers. We are only just beginning to uncover how people come to feel loved and whether that experience can be changed—if it can be learned, like a skill.

In our research on shared beliefs about love, we asked 495 people representative of the U.S. population to judge situations that would make most people feel loved. What they ranked most love-eliciting was being shown compassion in difficult times—an act that, in principle, any person could (and should) offer. Pets feeling happy to see them ranked highly for eliciting the feeling of being loved. Clearly, love, as people live it day to day, reaches past courtship and partnership.

Work like this partly drives how we measure who feels loved and when. It is common for studies to measure the experience of love as if it were a stable trait—for example, romantic attachment as a demographic indicator—but this practice obscures fundamental aspects of the lived human story. Relationship status may describe part of someone’s circumstances, but it does not tell us whether that person feels loved in their daily experience. For example, many people experience love while unattached, and many do not experience love despite being in a long-term relationship. In fact, in our studies, strong experiences of felt love are reported by participants who identify as unattached. To study felt love, we also have to ask about the experience directly.

Subjective states like felt love are inherently volatile, so studying it requires many careful observations, ideally while people are going about their daily lives. We often rely on complex research methods that focus not only on how people feel at a given time, but on the patterns of change over time and how those are shaped by our environment and affected by others.   Our basic approach is simple in principle: Ask people how loved they feel at different times while they go about their lives. In two studies published in 2020, in which over 200 participants reported their felt love states a handful of times per day for several weeks, we found that people with higher levels of felt love also tended to report greater flourishing and emotional well-being.

Frequent, repeated observations also allow us to disentangle a person’s typical experience from day-to-day fluctuations. This can also reveal individual differences in how persistent daily felt love states are—a feeling can be strong and brief, or weaker but sustained. Persistence is not automatically desirable: A below-usual feeling of being loved can persist, too. Whether particular patterns directly affect well-being remains an important open question.

To study the role of persistence, we developed custom mathematical models that include love inertia—that is, how long that warm feeling stays with us. In our recently published study, 52 participants reported how loved they felt as well as how much love they had expressed over a period of 28 days. We found that people whose felt love states were more persistent also enjoyed better mental health and indicators of well-being. Even more interestingly, the more people expressed love to others in their daily lives, the more they themselves tended to feel loved in return. Expressing love appears to be a gift that keeps on giving: Showing love is associated with a lasting sense of warmth and better well-being, and it also spreads love around you.

This finding suggests an actionable insight: Expressing love could be one route to feeling loved. If you are feeling a lack of connection, proactively expressing selfless care for someone else might help jumpstart your own feelings of being loved. We don’t yet know what accounts for this association. Expressing love might invite a warm response, change how you interpret a relationship, or be part of an interaction that lifts both people up.

Can an interaction actually change how loved someone feels? A 2024 study with 218 parent–child pairs argues just that. The authors of that study randomly assigned parents and their children, aged eight to 13, to one of two nine-minute conversations. In one condition, they took turns answering questions that encouraged personal disclosure (for example, instead of a surface-level question like “How was school?,” they might ask, “What is something you worry about?”). In the other condition, they made small talk. Children in the disclosure condition reported feeling more loved during the conversation.

This result cannot readily be explained by children in warmer families simply choosing more personal conversations—assignment of children to conditions was random. The researchers changed the conversation and observed a direct difference in the children’s experience.

Such findings need to be independently replicated for reliability and generalizability, but this is an important and promising step: A straightforward intervention changed an experience of feeling loved. The next question we can work on is whether changes of this kind can become part of daily life.

This piece is part of a three-year GGSC project to explore love's role in promoting social well-being, bridging divides, and contributing to a good, meaningful life.

Our focus on the receiver side of love brings a slightly more complex perspective: that an act does not produce a loving feeling by itself, but that effect is moderated by interpretation and expectation—thoughts that come to us in the moment, cognitive beliefs that support those thoughts, and the justifications we have for those beliefs. A colleague’s quiet help can be experienced as love, or dismissed as routine professional courtesy.

An area that is not yet well-explored is how the reception of expressions of love can be taught. If feelings follow perceptions, and perceptions follow beliefs, then felt love is a cognitive phenomenon in addition to an emotional one, so that cognitive skills contribute to how we experience the social world. It may be possible for people to learn to notice when they reflexively dismiss acts of care, to check their interpretation against what actually happened, and to re-evaluate in order to let the sender’s kindness count. It is also possible that a deficit in this cognitive skill prohibits many people from benefiting from the ambient love that is already present in everyday life. Even so, biased perceptions can have positive effects in this context. People with a stronger general tendency to interpret daily life experiences as expressions of love feel more loved in general, with all the well-being benefits that brings.

We now have ways to measure felt love in daily life, evidence linking it to well-being, and at least one experiment that changed it in the moment. A sensible next step is to connect those findings.

Can an intervention change daily feelings of love for weeks or months? Does that change lead to better well-being? Does it work for people who start out feeling relatively unloved, or mainly for those already doing well? Those are different questions, all embedded in the vastly complex network of our daily environments and experiences—part of what is sometimes called the exposome—and they require different, carefully drawn comparisons.

Future trials could focus on the cognitive component of felt love—whether an underlying ability to appreciate acts of love extends to other facets of life, and whether interventions should target the individual’s reasoning before focusing on their social context. Repeated measurements in daily-life settings could be used to show whether any differences generated by the intervention survive after the study session. Such trials would need to be designed with a great deal of care to establish the mechanisms of causality—a standard challenge of daily-life social psychology studies, but an especially critical one here. Establishing how to make experiences of felt love more available is an expansive but worthwhile scientific endeavor.

Finally, an emerging focus on love also recognizes how it is culturally and contextually bound. While certain loving gestures are found all over the world (such as helping someone in need, or empathic attention), to foster intercultural understanding, empathy, and inclusivity in diverse societies, it is vital to recognize love’s cultural variations in everyday life. We are currently conducting global cross-cultural research to identify specific ways love is shown in everyday life across different types of relationships, beyond just the romantic.

While we and other researchers continue to conduct larger studies to unpack these exact mechanisms, we don’t have to wait for the data to start paying closer attention to the quiet moments in our own lives. What happens when you feel genuinely cared for? What happens when you proactively express love to someone else? Being mindful of how they respond can reveal something that checking a box to count affectionate gestures misses. Fostering these experiences doesn’t require a laboratory—it is a daily practice we can all begin right now. Showing love is a low-cost experiment we can all conduct in our own daily lives.

Zita Oravecz, Ph.D., is a professor of human development and family studies at Penn State and a faculty co-hire in the Institute for Computational and Data Sciences. An expert in quantitative psychology, she develops Bayesian and dynamic statistical models that show how shared beliefs and individual feelings change over time.

Joachim Vandekerckhove, Ph.D., is a professor and chancellor’s fellow at the University of California, Irvine, where he has appointments at the Department of Cognitive Sciences, Department of Statistics, and the Department of Logic & Philosophy of Science. He is an internationally recognized expert in statistical and experimental methodology as well as in cognitive and behavioral modeling, and lectures and consults on these topics for clients in the academic, public, and private sector.

#3936. How Young People Are Researching Issues That Matter to Them

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Become a subscribing member today. Help us continue to bring “the science of a meaningful life” to you and to millions around the globe.

Greater Good Science Center • Magazine • In Action • In Education

“Adults should take students more seriously, as they have voices and different opinions too” — Middle school student

Middle school students in a California school district noticed that their perspectives were not included in decisions related to school policy that they cared about, like the dress code. They engaged in research to explore how administrators, teachers, and staff could incorporate their voices in decision-making processes. Students conducted surveys and focus groups with their peers and developed recommendations for their school, including more student-led events and a more active role engaged in school policy discussions. The following semester, the principal added students to several school leadership committees, where they helped inform programs and policies beyond the dress code, such as student social events and classroom curricular activities.

Many youth across the U.S. report feeling disengaged and disconnected from their schools, which is associated with declining attendance and greater mental health challenges. Addressing these challenges is critical, especially because a sense of belonging and connectedness predicts attendance, academic success, social well-being, self-esteem, and other positive outcomes for students.

Research is one important way to seek out solutions to these problems, and Youth Participatory Action Research (YPAR) is an innovative and growing approach that engages youth as experts to improve their lives, communities, and youth-serving institutions. In middle and high schools across the U.S., students in YPAR classes conduct research to inform policies, practices, and peer education efforts. They have prioritized and addressed a wide range of academic and school-climate issues, such as improving teacher-student relationships, peer relationships, safety, and school lunch options; and reducing disproportionate discipline, substance use, and transit barriers.

In schools that provide opportunities for YPAR, students are trained to take up roles as researchers, knowledge producers, and changemakers who contribute to their school communities. Further, YPAR evidence can deepen adults’ understanding of what shapes students’ feelings of belonging, patterns of engagement, and well-being. YPAR findings thus can inform more relevant and responsive solutions that can benefit youth, adults, and the broader community. This article provides guidance for educators who work with K–12 students and are interested in implementing YPAR.

Here are three examples of how YPAR impacts different levels of the school “ecosystem.”

Systems-level: High school YPAR students in an Ohio school district explored whether their school provided adequate mental health services. They conducted a schoolwide survey, and their findings revealed that although there were mental health services available, many students were not aware of them. In response, the student researchers took action and developed more promotional materials and held informational assemblies to raise awareness of these services. They also held meetings with teachers, counselors, administrators, and the superintendent to discuss potential districtwide solutions to improve mental health services and access.

Relationships: Middle school YPAR students in a California school district identified that they didn’t feel supported by their teachers. To explore this issue further, they conducted surveys and focus groups with their peers on teacher-student relationships. Their findings offered concrete suggestions on how teachers could improve their mentorship relationships with students, including practicing active listening, showing respect and compassion, being more flexible with assignments, and incorporating more engaging classroom activities that lift up student voices. The students also recommended student-led teacher evaluations.

Student-level: High school YPAR students in a California school district conducted English and Spanish focus groups with their peers on attendance and belonging. Based on their findings, the YPAR students proposed buddy programs to connect new students with their peers, schoolwide workshops to raise awareness on racial stereotyping, and lunchtime soccer tournaments to increase a sense of belonging.

Our YPAR Design Guide offers several considerations when deciding whether YPAR is a fit for your program or setting. YPAR has been primarily implemented with middle and high school students, and is well-suited to strengthening the developmental capacities of youth in early, middle, and late adolescence. However, elementary school students have also engaged in successful YPAR projects using mapping and arts-based methods.

First, adult facilitators (for example, teachers or program leaders) should assess capacity and consider whether there are adequate resources to pursue the YPAR project, including the timeframe of the project, staffing required, whether funding is needed, and the setting and scope.

Second, adult facilitators can introduce YPAR to students in a way that meets them “where they are at.” Not every student will be interested in YPAR from the start. Some may have had limited exposure to research, and others may not have had positive experiences (for example, through a particularly challenging or frustrating class assignment). Facilitators can engage the students in activities that lift up their experiences, then generate excitement by framing research as a tool for exploring and taking action on the issues that students care about.

Explore compassion, self-compassion, and awe with educators around the world.

Facilitators should also work with students to clarify their motivations for the project: What is the purpose of the project? What do students hope to get out of participating in YPAR? And what does their desired impact look like? In several projects, we have seen students become more interested in YPAR as they move through the process, analyze data, and develop their action plans and recommendations.

Finally, adult facilitators should be attentive to the broader context surrounding the topic or issue. For example, if the research is on safety and well-being, what programs or initiatives are already focusing on these issues, both in and out of school (for example, community health organizations)? What are the relevant constraints (for example, barriers accessing decision-making processes)? And where might there be opportunities to partner and collaborate? Understanding these factors will help adult facilitators and students choose an area of focus that aligns with their goals.

YPAR is not “one size fits all.” Adult YPAR facilitators adapt YPAR across a multitude of settings, including schools, afterschool programs, and community-based organizations such as youth centers. Even within schools, a YPAR process can vary in length. For example, some YPAR projects span a single unit within a semester; others may take up an entire semester, and in some cases extend across a full academic year (fall and spring semesters).

Further, YPAR is sometimes conducted as part of a club or extracurricular activity outside of formal class time. While the structure and duration of YPAR projects vary, many projects follow these steps: building the team, identifying and defining an issue or problem, selecting a research design and methods, collecting data, analyzing and interpreting results, developing action plans and recommendations, disseminating findings, and taking action.

Throughout the YPAR process, young people serve as leaders who decide on the topic, issue, and questions they wish to investigate. Adult facilitators serve as supportive guides and mentors, training them in research methods focused on generating numerical data or text- and image-based data. Youth then go on to collect and analyze their data and develop actions plans and recommendations to address their selected problem.

You can learn more about YPAR practice and research by accessing the following resources: YPAR Hub: One-stop shop for YPAR lesson plans and other resources YPAR Network: A broad network of diverse YPAR scholars and practitioners that will expand and refine YPAR practice, and increase the reach, evidence base, and impact of YPAR. Locate our YPAR online training modules here. IRB resources for researchers who want to study YPAR and a supplemental article

YPAR Hub: One-stop shop for YPAR lesson plans and other resources

YPAR Network: A broad network of diverse YPAR scholars and practitioners that will expand and refine YPAR practice, and increase the reach, evidence base, and impact of YPAR. Locate our YPAR online training modules here.

IRB resources for researchers who want to study YPAR and a supplemental article

A core value in YPAR is authentic power-sharing between adult facilitators and youth researchers. Most of the time, youth navigate learning spaces where they are “receivers” of knowledge from adults. Further, in traditional adult-led research projects, youth typically serve only as “respondents” to surveys or interviews, with no say in the research questions, methods, or interpretation.

The YPAR approach challenges these dynamics by positioning youth as knowledge generators who combine lived-experience expertise with systematic data-based inquiry. Youth researchers have decision-making power and are primary drivers of the YPAR process, while adults serve as guides to help them in advancing their projects.

Power-sharing is a learnable and teachable set of skills. Our guide provides lessons and activities that help facilitators attend to power dynamics, along with strategies that support power-sharing throughout YPAR. Adults and youth can productively share power in research when supportive structures are in place.

The results from YPAR research may (or may not) result in policy or practice change. Adult facilitators play a critical role in strategically connecting YPAR evidence to key decision-making spaces and helping implement youth action plans.

One step that facilitators can take is identifying existing decision-making structures where there may be opportunities to integrate YPAR evidence. They can also determine whether new routines need to be established. High School youth in Colorado investigated practices (through student surveys) that they noticed enhanced student-teacher relationships. Students then developed and led a day-long professional development for teachers on how small adjustments to the start of the year, including taking time to build trust and rapport before reviewing the syllabi, could increase student engagement and buy-in.

In our work, students have consistently emphasized how much it matters for adults to “keep them in the loop,” reporting back on whether and how their YPAR evidence was used. Authentically integrating YPAR evidence involves being transparent with students and following up with them throughout the process. Adults should take YPAR evidence seriously and actively prevent tokenizing processes—in other words, where YPAR evidence becomes a check-the-box rather than meaningful engagement. Ultimately, adults supporting YPAR must take an active role in creating conditions where youth feel safe, heard, and valued.

There is growing interest in YPAR at the local, state, and national levels, and it aligns with many initiatives that are already happening in schools. For example, the National Institutes of Health identified YPAR as a promising approach to address conditions that affect mental health and well-being, such as social isolation and exposure to unsafe environments. In California, SB997 mandates student voice be integrated in school and district budgeting processes. Oregon, Ohio, and New Jersey have similar mandates that require youth voice in decision making. There are strong synergies between YPAR and social-emotional learning programs, which are implemented in many school sites. YPAR also aligns with many academic competencies and standards, offering students a unique opportunity to build durable academic and career skills.

Students care about their schools and want to improve them. Adults need to learn along with them, listen to them, value their ideas, and support them in making change.

#3855. Say What? 7 Tips to Unclog Your Blocked Ears

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Earwax buildup, allergies, infections, or changes in air pressure might be behind your clogged ears. There are many easy ways to get relief at home.

Whether it’s from a cold, allergies, or changes in air pressure, blocked ears can disrupt your day-to-day activities and even cause discomfort. Thankfully, there are several effective methods to help unclog your ears and restore your hearing. Listen up!

Why are my ears clogged?

Clogging can happen either on the inside or the outside of your eardrum. Here are the most common ways your ears can get blocked:

  • Earwax in the outer ear helps prevent particles and excess water from entering the middle ear. However, if earwax builds up too much outside the eardrum, it can cause a blockage.
  • If you’ve been swimming recently, some water might be stuck in your ear canal, pushing against your eardrum. When that water hangs around too long, it can lead to a swimmer’s ear, an outer ear infection.
  • Sinus infections, ear infections, and Eustachian tube dysfunction can pressure your eardrum’s inner side.
  • Allergies can cause uncomfortable inflammation and congestion in your ears. Your sinuses, throat, and ears are connected, so when you feel stuffed up and sniffly, your inner ear feels the pain too.
  • Changes in air pressure, like when you fly, can cause uncomfortable ear pressure.

Best clogged ear remedies

1. Pop and unclog

The easiest way to unclog your ears is by “popping” them. Just pinch your nose shut, seal your lips, and gently blow. This simple technique, known as the Valsalva maneuver, won’t clear earwax or fluid but is a common trick people use on planes.

2. Swallow, yawn, or chew

The Eustachian tubes connect your middle ear to the back of your throat. They briefly open when you swallow, yawn, or chew. Altitude changes can cause these tubes to close, but chewing gum or yawning can help reopen them. Alternatively, you can try the Toynbee maneuver, where you hold your nose and swallow.

3. Ear drops

Over-the-counter (OTC) ear drops can soften and dissolve excess earwax. If you prefer a DIY approach, warm a small amount of mineral oil and use an eye dropper to apply a few drops into your ear for a few minutes.

Pro tip: Baby oil or olive oil can also be used, but make sure it’s not too hot. Additionally, diluted hydrogen peroxide drops can be helpful.

4. Warmth and gravity

To loosen up clogged earwax, use a warm, damp washcloth as a compress. Lie down with the clogged ear facing down against the heated washcloth. Gravity and heat will help the earwax move along more easily.

5. Hit the steam room

Steam can help drain sinuses and alleviate pressure in your inner ear. Close the bathroom door, let the hot shower run for a while, and then luxuriate with deep, cleansing breaths. 

6. Take allergy medication

Because your throat, sinuses, and ears are interconnected, any sickness affecting your sinuses or throat can also impact your ears.

Over-the-counter antihistamines, decongestants, or nasal sprays can relieve sniffles, coughs, and ear discomfort.

7. Rinse away the gunk

Rinsing your nasal passages with a medication-free saline solution can help reduce congestion and clear out mucus that might be causing ear blockage. You can also consider investing in a nasal irrigation system for more thorough mucus clearing.

When to see your doctor

Have you been unable to unclog your ears for a few days, or are you experiencing severe pain, fever, dizziness, hearing loss, fluid discharge, or unusual odor? If so, it’s crucial to contact your healthcare provider promptly. These symptoms may indicate an infection that needs medical attention.

What not to do when you have clogged ears

  • Cotton swabs: Q-tips and other cotton swabs to remove earwax can push it further into your ear canal, so don’t do it. 
  • Candling: Ear candles might seem like a fun party trick, but there’s no science to prove they work, and they’ve been shown to cause injuries.
  • Sprays: Decongestant nasal sprays can be helpful, but using them too long can lead to rebound congestion.
  • Drying solutions: A few drops of rubbing alcohol or a mixture of one part vinegar and one part alcohol can help dry out the ears when you have a swimmer’s ear. However, doing this regularly can lead to irritation.

Takeaway

Clogged ears are often caused by earwax buildup, allergies, infections, or changes in air pressure. You can use over-the-counter or DIY ear drops, irrigation kits, or warm compresses to unblock your ears. Antihistamines or nasal sprays can also help relieve clogs caused by allergies.

PSA: Consult a healthcare provider if your blocked ears are painful or at-home remedies don’t work.
