#2130. Structural basis for protocadherin-1 recognition by New World hantaviruses

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Thank you for visiting nature.com. You are using a browser version with limited support for CSS. To obtain the best experience, we recommend you use a more up to date browser (or turn off compatibility mode in Internet Explorer). In the meantime, to ensure continued support, we are displaying the site without styles and JavaScript.

Nature (2026) Cite this article

Hantaviruses are rodent-borne pathogens that cause severe human disease for which no approved therapeutics are available. New World hantaviruses, including Andes virus (ANDV), use the cell-surface receptor protocadherin-1 (PCDH1) to enter host cells1. However, the structural basis of this interaction remains unclear. Here we describe the structure of PCDH1 bound to the ANDV spike at near-atomic resolution. PCDH1 asymmetrically engages a deep central pocket formed by four Gn protomers in the spike, explaining why only intact viral particles or soluble tetramers recognize it. Mutagenesis identified three Gn loops that shape the receptor-binding site and afford the transfer of PCDH1 binding from New World to Old World glycoproteins. Structural and functional analyses of four neutralizing antibodies revealed that they block receptor binding through two distinct mechanisms—occluding access to the PCDH1-binding site or sterically preventing receptor engagement. Together, these findings define the structural determinants of hantavirus–receptor recognition, reveal how a virus can mask its receptor-binding site by recessing it in a surface depression and how antibodies can counter this strategy, and provide a framework for the design of antiviral therapeutics targeting the hantavirus spike.

Hantaviruses are a large and globally distributed family of viruses associated with severe zoonotic disease in humans. The New World hantaviruses (NWHs) ANDV and Sin Nombre virus (SNV) cause hantavirus cardiopulmonary syndrome (HCPS) in South and North America, respectively, with high case-fatality rates that can approach 40%. Cardiogenic shock and respiratory failure are hallmarks of HCPS. Old World hantaviruses (OWHs) are endemic in Europe and Asia or globally distributed in some cases (Seoul virus (SEOV)). The OWH Hantaan virus (HTNV), Puumala virus (PUUV) and Dobrava-Belgrade virus (DOBV) are associated with severe zoonotic disease and considerable mortality. Although hantaviruses are typically transmitted to humans through inhalation of aerosolized excreta from infected rodents, ANDV appears uniquely capable of human-to-human transmission2,3, raising its global public health significance and epidemic potential. This risk was underscored by the recent ANDV outbreak on a cruise ship departing Ushuaia, Argentina4. No US Food and Drug Administration (FDA)- or European Medicines Agency (EMA)-approved medical countermeasures are available to prevent or treat hantaviral infections.

Hantavirus glycoprotein spikes are formed by two glycoproteins, Gn and Gc, which are organized into a partial lattice at the surface of the virion. Each spike consists of four Gn molecules that form a central stabilizing core surrounded by four Gc molecules at the periphery. Gc mediates the interspike contacts that stabilize the lattice. Gn consists of a head (GnH) and a base (GnB) domain. GnH is exposed at the viral surface and is thought to participate in receptor binding, whereas GnB is buried within the spike and mediates most of the intraspike interactions. Gc consists of three domains that drive membrane fusion. At neutral pH, GnH interacts with Gc, forming a pre-fusion complex that prevents premature fusion. After exposure to acidic pH, the Gn–Gc complex dissociates, allowing Gc to insert three hydrophobic loops into the host cell membrane and undergo conformational rearrangements that drive fusion of viral and cellular membranes. Structural studies have reported the structures of GnH, GnB, Gc, the GnH–Gc complex and the Gn–Gc spike from several hantaviruses5,6,7,8,9,10.

Understanding how hantaviruses recognize their cellular receptors is a critical step toward the rational development of therapies and vaccines. Previous studies have identified several cell-surface molecules as candidate entry host factors, including integrins β1–3 (refs. 11,12,13,14), decay accelerating factor (DAF, encoded by CD55)15,16, complement factors (gC1qR)17 and the cadherin-superfamily protein PCDH1 (ref. 1). However, only PCDH1 has been shown to directly recognize the ANDV Gn–Gc spike1,18 and to be required for infection of human endothelial cells—major targets of hantavirus infection1,19. Furthermore, PCDH1 is critical for ANDV infection in vivo and for disease pathogenesis in a lethal hamster model of ANDV challenge1,18. PCDH1 is a type I membrane protein expressed in human airway epithelial and endothelial cells. It contains seven extracellular cadherin-repeat (EC) domains that extend into the extracellular space. Binding studies have shown that PCDH1 does not recognize soluble monomeric glycoproteins but instead binds to the tetrameric glycoprotein spikes displayed on the viral surface1,18. Monoclonal antibodies targeting the first EC repeat (EC1) inhibited entry of a recombinant vesicular stomatitis virus (rVSV) pseudotyped with Gn–Gc from multiple NWHs1, and point mutations at the tip of EC1 prevented PCDH1’s interaction with Gn–Gc of rVSVs bearing the glycoproteins of ANDV (rVSV-ANDV)1,18. Together, these findings indicate that NWHs infect host cells by engaging the EC1 domain of PCDH1. However, the structural determinants of this interaction remain unclear. Here we sought to determine the structure of PCDH1 bound to the ANDV Gn–Gc spike.

PCDH1 binds to viral particles but not to the soluble GnH–Gc heterodimer1,18, suggesting that the receptor-binding site is presented only on viral spikes. To elucidate the structural basis for PCDH1 recognition, we designed a soluble version of the ANDV spike tetramer by adding a hetero-octamerization domain at the C-terminal end of the Gn and Gc ectodomains (Fig. 1a). The octamerization sequence was derived from HALC4_136, a de novo tetramerization domain comprising three antiparallel helices per protomer20. We split this scaffold by fusing the first two helices to Gn and the third helix to Gc, so that co-expression of Gn and Gc would reconstitute a hetero-oligomeric assembly with the stoichiometry of the native spike (Extended Data Fig. 1a). We cloned both genes into a bicistronic plasmid, purified the complex from the supernatants of stably transfected Drosophila melanogaster S2 cells using affinity and size-exclusion chromatography and confirmed that it interacts with a truncated PCDH1 construct consisting of wild-type EC domains 1 to 4 (sEC1–4WT) using an enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay (ELISA; Fig. 1b). As a negative control, we used a construct containing the substitutions Y81A and F83A (sEC1–4MUT), which are known to abrogate virus binding18. We also confirmed that incubation of the soluble spike with an excess of soluble sEC1WT, but not sEC1MUT, prevented the binding to sEC1–4WT (Fig. 1c). Furthermore, we used biolayer interferometry (BLI) to demonstrate specific spike–EC1 binding. Specifically, sEC1–4WT or sEC1–4MUT were immobilized on sensors, which were then dipped into solutions containing increasing concentrations of soluble spike (Fig. 1d). BLI assays performed in the opposite orientation corroborated these findings (Extended Data Fig. 1b).

a, Schematic of the soluble spike construct (top). The Gn and Gc ectodomains were fused to a C-terminal tetramerization domain (TD). Cysteine residues in the Gn and Gc TD domains were engineered to form a disulfide bond. N-glycosylation sites are shown in green, and the location of the signal peptides (SP) is indicated. Bottom, schematic of the construct used to produce the eVLPs fused to the PCDH1 EC1 domain. b, ELISA binding of soluble spikes to immobilized WT or double-mutant PCDH1 EC1–4 (DM: Y81A/F83A). Data are mean ± s.d. from four independent experiments, two performed in triplicate and two in duplicate (n = 10). c, EC1 competition ELISA. Soluble spike tetramers were preincubated with serial dilutions of WT or DM sEC1 before addition to WT PCDH1 EC1–4-coated plates. Data are mean ± s.d. from two independent experiments performed in triplicate (n = 6). d, BLI analysis of soluble spike tetramer binding to His-tagged WT or DM sEC1–4 immobilized on NTA biosensors. One representative experiment is shown from three independent experiments. e, Cryo-EM maps were obtained after co-expression of the soluble spike tetramers with PCDH1 EC1–4. Left, top view along the four-fold axis. Right, the same map rotated 45° around the y axis. Gn is shown in red, Gc in yellow and PCDH1 EC1 in blue. f, Atomic model of ANDV spike bound to PCDH1 EC1, coloured as in e. g, Contacts of Gn and PCDH1 EC1. Gn is shown in pink and PCDH1 EC1 in light blue; Gn loops AB (violet), CD (dark green) and DE (red) or PCDH1 EC1 loops BC (blue) and FG (light green) are highlighted as indicated in the figure. h, Cryo-EM map obtained by fusing the PCDH1 EC1 domain to the N terminus of the M segment, coloured as in e and as indicated in the bottom schematic of a.

To verify that the soluble spike proteins fold like the spikes observed on the viral surface, we performed cryo-electron microscopy (cryo-EM). We observed a few two-dimensional (2D) classes compatible with Gn–Gc tetramers (Extended Data Fig. 1c). However, a low-resolution three-dimensional (3D) reconstruction obtained from them was not compatible with the known ANDV spike structure5 (Extended Data Fig. 1d), suggesting that, in the absence of lateral contacts between spikes and the transmembrane regions, the intersubunit contacts within the spike are not sufficiently stable to maintain the intact eight-subunit assembly. We next hypothesized that prolonged incubation of the two proteins might help to stabilize the spike in its receptor-binding conformation. To test this hypothesis, we co-transfected S2 cells with the plasmids encoding sEC1–4WT and the soluble spike and purified the complex using double-affinity and size-exclusion chromatography. We performed cryo-EM studies and observed many new 2D classes showing tetramers (Extended Data Fig. 2 and Extended Data Table 1). Using particles from these classes, we reconstructed a map at a resolution of 2.4 Å, and we used the map to build a model of the spike that closely resembles the tetramers observed on viral particles5. The only notable differences between the two models are a slight rotation of the GnH domains relative to GnB and the poorly resolved density for domain III in the soluble spikes (Extended Data Fig. 3).

The cryo-EM map of the complex revealed an additional density at the centre of the spike, consistent in size with a single EC domain of PCDH1. However, as the map was processed using C4 symmetry, and the symmetry axis passes through the centre of the spike, we were unable to model this residual density. We reprocessed the map by combining a masked 3D classification with a non-uniform refinement using a pseudo-C4 symmetry. This approach yielded a new map at a resolution of 2.6 Å (Fig. 1e), in which we modelled PCDH1 EC1 at the centre of the spike (Fig. 1f,g). The final model showed the EC1 domain deeply inserted into a pocket formed by four protomers of Gn, establishing contacts with two neighbouring protomers (660 and 200 Å2, respectively) and sparse contacts with the other two (80 Å2 and 35 Å2). This mode of interaction, whereby one receptor molecule interacts with four viral glycoproteins, represents a binding mode that to our knowledge has not been observed in any enveloped virus, and explains why PCDH1 binds to viral particles but not to soluble GnH–Gc heterodimers. In the model, EC1 loops BCEC1 and FGEC1 interact with the AB and CD loops of GnH and the DE loop of GnB from two neighbouring protomers (Fig. 1g and Extended Data Fig. 4a). Notably, the densities corresponding to the DE and CD loops are weaker in the protomers that do not engage EC1 than in the receptor-contacting protomers (Extended Data Fig. 3b). This suggests that these loops are flexible and that PCDH1 binding stabilizes specific conformations of these regions. Indeed, comparison of the four protomers reveals that EC1 binding induces a conformational change in the CD and DE loops of one protomer, creating asymmetry within the pocket (Extended Data Fig. 3b). Previous studies have identified PCDH1 mutations that inhibit virus binding18, and all are in the BCEC1 loop or nearby regions, probably altering the conformation of the loop. Consistent with the structural data, mutations in the tip of the FG loop had little (D142A, D142R) or no (L143A, V144A, Q145A) effect on virus binding18, probably because none of them introduce steric restrictions (Fig. 1g).

To validate the model with viral particles, we studied the interaction between PCDH1 and virus-like particles (VLPs). We did not attempt to decorate preformed VLPs with soluble receptor because flow cytometry revealed only weak binding of EC1 to ANDV glycoproteins presented on the cell surface. Instead, reasoning that increasing the local concentration of receptor near the spike could enhance binding, we fused EC1 to the N terminus of the M segment through a flexible linker (Fig. 1a). To increase particle production, we introduced an EABR tag21 and the V535K and S1096L mutations, as reported previously5,22, and transfected the construct into FreeStyle293 cells. We analysed the secreted particles using cryo-EM and obtained a reconstruction of the spike at an overall resolution of 3.9 Å (Extended Data Fig. 5). The map revealed clear densities for Gn and Gc and an additional density at the centre of the spike (Fig. 1h). This central density was compatible in size and position with the PCDH1 EC1 domain observed in the soluble spike complex. However, the quality of this density did not allow unambiguous building of the EC1 domain, preventing a detailed comparison of the VLP–receptor interface with that obtained using the soluble spike (Extended Data Fig. 5c). This work nevertheless confirmed that PCDH1 EC1 occupies the central region of the Gn–Gc tetramer in viral particles.

To begin to investigate the roles of the CD and AB loops of GnH and the DE loop of GnB in spike–PCDH1 recognition, we produced rVSV-ANDV and rVSVs bearing the glycoproteins of the PCDH1-independent1,19 OWH HTNV (rVSV-HTNV), as well as rVSV-ANDV variants in which we replaced the GnH CD loop or all three loops in contact with the receptor (Methods and Extended Data Fig. 4a) with those from HTNV. After normalizing the amount of incorporated Gn–Gc in the viral particles (Extended Data Fig. 4b), we measured their interaction with immobilized PCDH1 EC1 on ELISA plates. Consistent with the known receptor specificity of hantaviruses and our structural data, PCDH1 EC1 could capture rVSV-ANDV but not rVSV-HTNV or any of the chimeric variants (Fig. 2a).

a, ELISA binding of PCDH1 EC1–2 to rVSV particles bearing hantavirus envelope glycoproteins. Plates coated with sEC1–2 were incubated with serial dilutions of rVSV expressing ANDV or HTNV glycoproteins, or chimeric ANDV glycoproteins containing the HTNV loop CD or HTNV loops CD, AB and DE, as indicated. The starting dilution of each rVSV chimera was normalized to the parental hantavirus based on its Gn–Gc expression. The ELISA signal was normalized to rVSV-ANDV, and viruses were compared at the 4× viral dilution using two-way analysis of variance (ANOVA) with Tukey’s multiple-comparison test; the reference is shown in bold. Data are mean ± s.d. n = 6 wells per viral particle dilution from three independent experiments (n = 5 wells for the 4× dilution of ANDV). ****P < 0.0001 (ANDV versus ANDVHTNV-all, ANDVHTNV-CDLoop, or HTNV: P = 1.1 × 10−12). b, DMS analysis of PCDH1 binding to an alphavirus-based pseudotyped SHV. SHV particles bearing ANDV glycoproteins were generated from a Gn library containing all amino acid substitutions at each targeted residue. Particles were captured on PCDH1 EC1–2-coated ELISA plates, and the variant abundance in the input and two PCDH1-bound replicates was quantified. Left, Benjamini–Hochberg FDR-corrected significance versus fold change (FC) for each variant in the PCDH1-bound fraction. Right, FC values were mapped onto the ANDV spike. Gc is shown in light yellow, and Gn is coloured according to the largest absolute FC value per residue, as indicated. c, Mapping of high-impact mutations. PCDH1 EC1 is shown as a surface representation, Gn as a cartoon with loops CD and AB and helix α2 coloured green, magenta and pink, respectively. In the right panel, the receptor surface is coloured according to electrostatic potential. Selected residues are shown as sticks, with the corresponding mutations indicated and numbered as in b. An upward arrow indicates that the mutation is enriched in the selection, whereas a downward arrow indicates that it is depleted.

Next, we used a deep-mutational scanning (DMS) approach to more comprehensively survey the sequence landscape of the spike–PCDH1 interaction. We generated two independent Gn libraries, each introducing all possible amino acid substitutions at 427 of 448 positions in the ectodomain (amino acids 24–471; Methods) and used these to create libraries of alphavirus-based vectors pseudotyped with ANDV Gn–Gc (Sem-Hantavirus (SHV)). We captured SHVs bound to PCDH1 EC1 immobilized on ELISA plates and sequenced the PCDH1-bound SHV fractions to identify variants enriched or depleted relative to the input population. We observed strong selective signals for the GnH CD and AB loops and nearby regions in both populations, consistent with roles for these sequences in virus–receptor recognition (Fig. 2b,c and Extended Data Fig. 6). Relatively few variants were recovered from the GnB DE loop, consistent with its higher level of conservation (Extended Data Fig. 4a) and suggesting an important structural role for this sequence in the assembly and/or stability of the spike. Notably, none of the Gn ectodomain substitutions observed in the recent Hondius outbreak isolate of ANDV (LS-CH2016/2016-12-23/Switzerland) mapped to the PCDH1-binding pocket or were selected in the DMS experiments, indicating that this virus probably resembles previous isolates of the virus in its ability to recognize and use PCDH1 (Extended Data Figs. 4 and 6).

PCDH1 is a specific receptor for NWHs, and we wondered whether the structure of the complex could reveal insights into receptor use. We analysed the sequence conservation of the PCDH1-binding pocket across hantaviruses and compared the conformation of the loops using the available crystal structures of HTNV and SNV. We identified two amino acid substitutions: one in the GnH AB loop of SEOV, DOBV and HTNV (L36F) and one in the GnB DE loop of HTNV (G418L) that are predicted to cause steric clashes with PCDH1. By contrast, both the sequence and structure of the GnH CD loop vary substantially across hantaviruses—it is an extended loop in NWHs but a compact helix in OWHs (Fig. 3a and Extended Data Fig. 4). These findings suggest that it is not only the residues directly contacting PCDH1 that are important to the spike–receptor interaction but also nearby residues that influence the overall conformation of the loops, as is also indicated by the DMS data (Fig. 2b and Extended Data Fig. 6).

a, Structural comparison of the PCDH1-interacting region in NWHs and OWHs. Top, alignments of the CD loop from ANDV (red), SNV (dark blue; Protein Data Bank (PDB): 8AHN)45 and HTNV (yellow; PDB: 6Y6P). Aromatic residues in NWHs are shown as sticks. Bottom, alignment of the loop AB from ANDV (red) and HTNV (yellow; PDB: 6Y6P)9. b, ELISA binding of rVSV particles bearing hantavirus glycoproteins to soluble PCDH1-EC1–2-coated plates. The superscript notes indicate loop substitutions: CD, loop CD; All, loops CD, AB and DE. The starting dilutions of the rVSV-chimeras were normalized to their parental hantavirus based on Gn–Gc expression. The ELISA signal was normalized to rVSV-ANDV, and viruses were analysed at the 4× dilution using two-way ANOVA with Tukey’s multiple-comparison test; references are shown in bold. Data are mean ± s.d. n = 6 wells per viral particle dilution from three independent experiments, except for the ANDV–HTNV comparison of ANDV (n = 5 for the 4× viral dilution) and HTNVANDV-all (n = 4 wells per viral dilution from two independent experiments, and n = 2 for the 1× viral dilution; no error bar is shown for this datapoint). NS, not significant (P > 0.05), ****P < 0.0001. c, Inhibition of rVSV infection by soluble PCDH1 EC1–2 (sEC1–2WT, top row; sEC1–2MUT (F83A/D85R), bottom row). The infectivity of each virus was normalized to that obtained at the lowest concentration of sEC1–2WT or in the absence of protein (sEC1–2MUT), with the corresponding control set to 100%. The chimeric and parental rVSVs were compared using two-way ANOVA with Tukey’s multiple-comparison test at the 4× protein dilution. Data are mean ± s.e.m. n = 10 wells from five independent experiments (sEC1–2WT and sEC1–2MUT), except for SNV (n = 8 wells for the 4× protein dilution) and ANDV and HTNVANDV-all (n = 8 wells from four independent experiments). *P < 0.05, **P < 0.01, ****P < 0.0001. Exact P values are provided in the Methods.

To assess the functional impact of these sequence variations, we compared PCDH1-binding rVSVs displaying the glycoproteins of ANDV (rVSV-ANDVWT), SNV (rVSV-SNVWT) and HTNV (rVSV-HTNVWT), along with variants in which we swapped the PCDH1-binding loops and nearby residues (Fig. 3b,c and Extended Data Fig. 4a). After normalizing the amount of incorporated glycoprotein in the rVSVs (Extended Data Fig. 4b), we measured PCDH1 binding by ELISA, as described above (Fig. 3b). As we showed previously1,18, rVSV-ANDVWT bound strongly to PCDH1, rVSV-SNVWT bound less strongly and rVSV-HTNVWT did not bind. The phenotypic effects of exchanging the receptor-binding loops between ANDV and SNV were largely consistent with the structural and mutagenesis data. Specifically, ANDV Gn bearing the SNV GnH CD loop bound worse than rVSV-ANDVWT, whereas SNV Gn bearing GnH CD or all three receptor-binding loops from ANDV Gn bound better than rVSV-SNVWT. Notably, a chimeric rVSV-HTNV bearing all three receptor-binding loops from ANDV Gn, but not the GnH CD loop alone, could now bind to PCDH1, albeit relatively weakly. This result is consistent with the sequence differences between ANDV and HTNV in the GnH AB and GnB DE loops and corroborates the proposed roles for these loops in ANDV spike–PCDH1 recognition.

The preceding binding experiments, in which sEC1–2 coated onto ELISA plates was used to capture rVSVs bearing hantavirus spikes, reflect the avid interaction between multiple immobilized PCDH1 molecules and the multiple spikes on each virion, which probably masks differences in the intrinsic affinity of each spike for PCDH1. Accordingly, we next assessed the ability of soluble, monomeric sEC1–2 to inhibit rVSV infection of PCDH1-bearing human umbilical vein endothelial cells (HUVECs) in receptor-competition experiments, as described previously18 (Fig. 3c). We established the specificity of this assay with a binding-incompetent double-mutant sEC1–2MUT as a negative control (Fig. 3c). As expected1,18, sEC1–2 inhibited infection by rVSVs bearing WT hantavirus spikes in a manner that was positively related to their capacity to recognize PCDH1: HTNVWT << SNVWT < ANDVWT. The sensitivities to PCDH1 inhibition of the ANDV-SNV chimeras (SNVWT ≈ ANDVSNV-All < ANDVSNV-CD < ANDVWT), the SNV-ANDV chimeras (SNVWT < SNVANDV-CD < SNVANDV-All < ANDVWT) and the HTNV-ANDV chimeras (HTNVWT ≈ HTNVANDV-CD < HTNVANDV-All < ANDVWT) were also concordant with our binding results. Taken together, these findings broadly support the conclusion that the sequences shaping the central cavity are the major determinants of hantavirus spike–receptor recognition and of the distinct receptor tropisms of NWHs and OWHs.

The PCDH1-binding cavity is poorly accessible to conventional antibodies. Its dimensions—approximately 2.5 × 2.5 nm at its upper opening, narrowing to around 2.2 × 2.2 nm at the bottom, and around 2.1 nm in depth—are incompatible with those of a Fab fragment (5 × 4 × 7 nm). This raises the question of how some neutralizing antibodies block receptor engagement. To examine their mechanisms of action, we analysed four well-studied human neutralizing antibodies: ANDV-5 (ref. 23) and MIB22 (refs. 23,24), which were reported to interfere with the binding of soluble PCDH1 to spike-expressing cells; ANDV-34 (ref. 23), which does not block this interaction; and ADI-65534 (ref. 10), which was reported to block the binding of rVSVs to PCDH1 EC1–2 immobilized on ELISA plates. Structures of ANDV-5, ANDV-34 and ADI-65534 are available, but no structure had been reported for MIB22. We therefore determined the crystal structure of an antibody fragment of MIB22 in a complex with ANDV GnH at a resolution of 3.1 Å (Extended Data Table 2) and identified an epitope of about 900 Å2 that includes the three previously identified escape mutations23. Notably, MIB22 contacts several residues in the CD loop and recognizes a conformation of the loop different from that adopted after PCDH1 binding (Extended Data Fig. 7a). To assess whether antibody binding occludes the PCDH1-binding pocket, we modelled the variable domains of the four neutralizing antibodies onto the spike–PCDH1 complex (Fig. 4a). In parallel, we produced the four neutralizing antibodies as both full-length antibodies (IgGs) and single-chain variable fragments (scFvs) lacking the antibody constant regions and compared their ability to block rVSV-ANDV–PCDH1 binding by ELISA (Fig. 4b). We also compared the neutralizing activity of the four full-length antibodies against rVSV-ANDV in HUVECs, which express higher levels of PCDH1 than many immortalized cell lines of non-endothelial origin1 (Fig. 4c).

a, Model of the ANDV spike bound to PCDH1 EC1–3 and four scFv fragments from the indicated neutralizing antibodies. Models were generated by aligning the PCDH1 EC1–3 (PDB: 6BX7) and the corresponding antigen/antibody structures: ADI-65534 (PDB: 9P3Y), ANDV-34 and ANDV-5 (PDB: 8DBZ), and MIB22 (this study) with the spike–EC1 complex. The spike and scFvs are shown as surface representations; Gn is shown in pink and Gc in light yellow. The heavy and light chains are shown in dark and light colours, respectively. PCDH1 is depicted as a cartoon and is coloured blue. The clashes between PCDH1 and the corresponding scFv are highlighted in the magnified panels. PCDH1 EC1 and EC2 are shown in blue and dark blue, respectively. Only two scFvs are displayed for clarity. b, Competition ELISA showing inhibition of rVSV-ANDV binding to PCDH1 by the indicated neutralizing antibodies. rVSV-ANDV was pre-incubated with antibodies in either IgG or scFv format for 1 h before being added to soluble PCDH1-EC1–2-coated ELISA plates. The ELISA signal was normalized to that obtained without competing IgGs or scFvs. Data are means ± s.d. n = 4 wells from two independent experiments. c, The capacity of neutralizing antibodies to inhibit viral entry. rVSV-ANDV was pre-incubated with each antibody (starting concentration, 33 nM) for 1 h before addition to HUVECs. Infectivity was normalized to that obtained at the lowest antibody concentration. Data are mean ± s.d. Infectivity curves were fitted to a four-parameter logistic (4PL) model. The IC50 and the residual infectivity at saturating antibody concentrations (Em) are shown. For ADI-65534, MIB22 and ANDV-34, n = 6 wells per antibody dilution examined over three independent experiments; for ANDV-5, n = 8 wells per antibody dilution examined over four independent experiments, except for the ninth antibody dilution (n = 6 wells).

We observed that ADI-65534 and ANDV-34 do not occlude access to the central pocket, suggesting that they compete with PCDH1 through a steric hindrance mechanism mediated by the antibody constant regions (Fig. 4a). Consistent with this model, ELISA-based competition binding experiments showed that scFvs did not block the interaction of rVSVs with the receptor, whereas the IgGs did (Fig. 4b). Consistent with these observations, ANDV-34 has been reported not to block binding to the soluble receptor in flow-cytometry-based assays25. By contrast, the models with the Fabs of ANDV-5 and MIB22 showed that these antibodies bind much closer to the centre of the spike, directly occluding the PCDH1-binding site. ANDV-5 may additionally make limited clashes with the neighbouring protomer, potentially perturbing the conformation of the tetrameric spike. Concordant with these findings and previous observations25, ANDV-5 and MIB22 interfere with rVSV-ANDV–PCDH1 binding as both full-length antibodies and scFvs (Fig. 4b). In our experiments, the scFv of ANDV-5 blocked receptor binding tenfold more efficiently than its MIB22 counterpart (half-maximum inhibitory concentration (IC50) values of 23 and 240 nM, respectively), which may be explained by ANDV-5’s higher intrinsic affinity for the spike23. Overall, these results revealed that neutralizing antibodies elicited during viral infection can block receptor binding either directly, by occluding the receptor-binding site, or indirectly, by sterically preventing PCDH1 from accessing the spike.

Here we determined the structure of the ANDV spike bound to human PCDH1. We found that the receptor inserts one membrane-distal EC1 domain into a deep cavity formed by four Gn molecules in the fully assembled spike tetramer.

PCDH1 is a calcium-dependent cell–cell adhesion protein. Biochemical studies with soluble ectodomain fragments showed that PCDH1 can exist in a monomer–dimer equilibrium in solution18,26; however, the oligomeric distribution of full-length PCDH1 at the plasma membrane is not known. Structural studies showed that PCDH1 can form antiparallel homodimers through an EC1–EC4 interface26—a configuration that is incompatible with the receptor-binding mode described here because EC4 would not fit into the pocket. Previous studies with recombinant PCDH1 mutants engineered to remain monomeric did not reduce their interaction with rVSV-ANDV, whereas stabilization of the dimer decreased binding18. Overall, these data suggest that the virus engages monomeric PCDH1 at the cell surface, although dynamic PCDH1 dimers may also contribute if they transiently expose the EC1 domain.

The binding mode reported here explains why PCDH1 recognizes spikes on viral particles but not soluble glycoproteins and shows that receptor binding depends on both glycoprotein sequence and spike organization. The concept that receptors can recognize quaternary surfaces formed only in assembled viral glycoprotein complexes is not unique to hantaviruses, as the receptor-binding pocket for multiple alphaviruses is accessible only in the complete spike. For example, western equine encephalitis virus (WEEV) can accommodate multiple receptors, including protocadherin-10 (PCDH10)27,28,29,30, the very-low-density lipoprotein receptor (VLDLR)29,30 and MXRA8 (refs. 27,31), using the same pocket formed by adjacent E2–E1 heterodimers. It is interesting to compare the interactions between ANDV and PCDH1 with those between WEEV and PCDH10 because both receptors are non-clustered δ-cadherins32 and use their membrane-distal EC1 domain for viral engagement. However, the two viruses use distinct binding modes and protocadherin-binding surfaces. PCDH10 EC1 engages the alphavirus spike laterally through its A, G, F and C β-strands, whereas PCDH1 EC1 inserts axially into the central cavity of the hantavirus spike using its BC and FG loops. By analogy with WEEV, we propose that the pockets analogous to the PCDH1-binding pocket of ANDV and other NWHs may serve as binding sites for the distinct receptors used by OWHs and other non-PCDH1-using hantaviruses. Consistent with this idea, we found that variations in the receptor-binding loops of HTNV Gn, which normally cannot recognize PCDH1, enable a rVSV displaying the HTNV spike to bind PCDH1. Structural, sequence and mutagenesis studies further showed that the GnH CD loop is flexible, variable and tolerant to multiple mutations, suggesting that it could adapt to recognize alternative receptors. DMS data indicated that substitutions in the GnH CD and AB loops can alter affinity for PCDH1. Furthermore, the fact that the pocket is formed by four subunits may also facilitate viral adaptation to different receptors through a limited number of amino acid substitutions.

Another interesting parallel with alphaviruses concerns fusion activation. In alphaviruses, the quaternary interactions that form the receptor-binding site must be disrupted to allow membrane fusion, and MXRA8 binding has been proposed to modulate the pH threshold for fusion activation33. By contrast, the PCDH1-binding site does not involve the hantavirus fusion protein Gc, suggesting that receptor binding and membrane fusion by hantaviruses might be independent events. However, it has been reported that Gn adopts a different tetrameric arrangement at acidic pH7, raising the possibility that PCDH1 engagement may also influence fusion activation.

The neutralizing antibody response is essential for controlling hantavirus infections, as neutralizing titres correlate with HCPS severity34 and administration of human ANDV immune plasma reduced the case fatality rate in a small trial35. Antibodies may neutralize enveloped viruses by several non-mutually exclusive mechanisms, including by blocking viral attachment, membrane fusion or egress, inducing virion aggregation and, in vivo, by recruiting Fc-dependent effector functions36,37. Several neutralizing monoclonal antibodies targeting the ANDV spike have been described23,24,38. Some block viral membrane fusion by preventing dissociation of the pre-fusion heterodimer or the formation of the post-fusion trimer. Others interfere with PCDH1 engagement, but the lack of structural information has prevented a mechanistic understanding of how they do so. The structural data presented here show that the PCDH1-binding pocket is too narrow to readily accommodate a conventional antibody. We therefore analysed a panel of four human neutralizing antibodies to understand how they block receptor binding. We observed that ADI-65534 and ANDV-34 do not directly compete with PCDH1 but instead inhibit receptor binding through a steric interference mechanism mediated by the antibody constant region. This model of neutralization, in which antibody binding to accessible spike surfaces can prevent virus attachment without directly targeting the receptor-binding sites, has been reported for other viruses37,39,40,41, and suggests that strategies to increase antibody occupancy at the virion surface, such as combining non-competing antibodies targeting accessible epitopes, or focusing the response on exposed loops in the most apical regions of the tetramer, may enhance neutralizing activity against hantaviruses.

In contrast to ADI-65534 and ANDV-34, ANDV-5 and MIB22 directly occlude the PCDH1-binding pocket by targeting epitopes adjacent to, but not within, the central cavity. This recessed receptor-binding site is reminiscent of the canyon-like clefts described in picornaviruses, which inspired Rossmann’s canyon hypothesis. In its original form, this hypothesis proposed that receptor-binding residues located deep within a narrow cleft could remain accessible to the receptor but not to conventional antibodies, thereby reducing the immune pressure on the most conserved residues42,43. However, the subsequent description43 of HRV14-specific neutralizing antibodies of which the Fabs can penetrate the receptor-binding cleft showed that virus–host arms races could indeed produce antiviral solutions to this strategy. The preceding observations with HRV14 raise an interesting alternative possibility also noted by these authors43: that the recessed receptor-binding site affords the virus a second line of defence by allowing it to escape neutralization through amino acid substitutions that disrupt a more extensive and surface-exposed virus–antibody interface while sparing a smaller and more buried virus–receptor interface. Indeed, ANDV escape mutations to MIB22 are at residues distinct from, and more peripheral to, those that are critical for ANDV–PCDH1 recognition. Our work suggests that access to conserved sequences at the base of the receptor-binding cavity in the hantavirus spike is likely to require antibodies with extended CDR loops, single-domain antibodies or biologics designed to fit into the cavity. The identification of such molecules could yield exceptionally potent and broad antiviral inhibitors. Finally, because PCDH1 binds to a pocket that is present only in the fully assembled spike and hantavirus glycoproteins are structurally dynamic44, it may also be possible to identify antibodies that recognize epitopes partially buried in the intact spike and thereby prevent formation of the receptor-binding site.

To produce the soluble spikes of ANDV, we inserted synthetic genes for Gn and Gc of ANDV (strain Chile-9717869, UniProt: Q9E006) codon-optimized for expression in Drosophila cells into a bicistronic plasmid to produce the plasmid p-sSpike. We designed the hetero-octamerization domain starting from HALC4_136, a synthetic homo-tetramerization domain formed by three antiparallel helices, which has the appropriate dimensions (PDB: 8D09)20. In the first cistron of the plasmid, we cloned the ectodomain of Gn (amino acids 21–483) followed by the first two helices of HALC4_136, which we refer to as HALC4_136N. The second cistron contains the complete ectodomain of Gc (amino acids 652–1107) followed by the third α-helix of HALC4_136, named HALC4_136C. To avoid aggregation, we introduced four mutations in Gc: F1089A, F1093A, L1100A and L1104A. To further stabilize the hetero-oligomerization domain, we designed a disulfide bond joining the C terminus of HALC4_136N with the N terminus of HALC4_136C. For purification purposes, we introduced a double strep-tag at the C terminus of HALC4_136C. For the structural studies, we cloned the extracellular cadherin domains EC1–4 of human PCDH1 (amino acids 58–503, UniProt: Q08174) into a pMT/BiP plasmid in frame with a His and a double-strep tag to produce the plasmid p-sEC1–4. We used these plasmids to obtain stable transfectants of Drosophila S2 cells (Thermo Fisher Scientific, R69007, authenticated by Thermo Fisher Scientific by karyotyping and isozyme profiling), together with the pCoPuro plasmid (ratio 1:20) for puromycin selection46. We generated stable cell lines with the p-sSpike (soluble spike) plasmid alone and cell lines co-transfecting p-sSpike and p-sEC1–4 (EC1–4). Stable cell lines were selected and maintained in LM-SFM4 medium (Cytiva) containing 8 μg ml−1 puromycin. Cultures of 1–3 l were grown in spinner flasks in medium supplemented with 1% penicillin–streptomycin antibiotics to approximately 107 cells per ml, and protein expression was induced with 4 μM CdCl2. After 5 days, the S2 medium supernatant was concentrated to 40 ml using a 10 kDa cut-off VivaFlow concentrator (Sartorius) and supplemented with Biolock (IBA) and 0.1 M Tris-HCl (pH 8.0), centrifuged for 30 min at 20,000g, and purified by Strep-Tactin affinity chromatography using a StrepTrap HP column (Cytiva). For the co-expression of soluble ANDV spikes and PCDH1 EC1–4, Strep-Tactin purification was followed by His affinity chromatography using the TALON column (Cytiva). Proteins were then purified by size-exclusion chromatography (SEC) on a 10/300 Superose6 Increase column (Cytiva) in 10 mM Tris pH 8.0, 100 mM NaCl. The protein purity and the presence of (Gn–Gc)4 spikes and PCDH1 in different SEC fractions after the co-expression were assessed by SDS–PAGE under reducing and non-reducing conditions.

To obtain the complex of PCDH1–EC1 with ANDV-VLPs, the nucleic acid sequence of the first extracellular domain (amino acids 58–165) of human protocadherin 1 plus a C-terminal 5 × GGGS linker were codon-optimized, synthesized by GenScript, and inserted into the pCAG-ANDV-DM-VLP plasmid5 to make the pCAG-PCDH1-EC1-GS5-ANDV-DM-VLP plasmid. The insertion was between the ANDV native signal peptide sequence (1–18), predicted by the SignalP 5.0 server47, and the N-terminal residue of Gn. For protein expression, the pCAG-PCDH1-EC1-GS5-ANDV-DM-VLP plasmid was mixed with 25 kDa polyethyleneimine (PEI; Polysciences) and transfected into FreeStyle 293-F cells (Thermo Fisher Scientific) at a density of 106 cells per ml. Transfected cells were incubated in a 37 °C shaking incubator with 8% CO2 and 80% humidity for 72 h. After transfection, the supernatants were collected by centrifugation, filtered with 0.22 μm Rapid-Flow filters (Nalgene), and concentrated to about 40 ml using a Centricon Plus-70 filter unit (MilliporeSigma). The concentrated supernatant was subsequently purified through discontinuous sucrose density gradient (20%, 50%, 70% sucrose in PBS) ultracentrifugation at 4°C, 135,000g for 2 h. Fractions corresponding to VLPs were collected, pooled and further concentrated to around 60 μl in PBS (pH 7.4) for cryo-EM grid preparation.

To obtain soluble His-tagged versions of human PCDH1 domains EC1–4 (sEC1–4), EC1–2 (sEC1–2) and EC1 (sEC1) for ELISA and BLI analyses, codon-optimized human PCDH1 fragments (amino acids 58–503 for sEC1–4, 58–284 for sEC1-2, and 58–160 for sEC1) were cloned into a pCAGGS (sEC1–4 and sEC1) or pcDNA3.1 (sEC1–2) plasmid in frame with a histidine tag. The sEC1–4 and sEC1 plasmids were used to transfect Expi293 (Thermo Fisher Scientific, A14527, not authenticated) at a density of 3 × 106 cells per ml using FectoPRO DNA transfection reagent (Polyplus). After 5 days of incubation at 37 °C, the supernatant was collected and centrifuged for 30 min at 3,100g. Proteins were purified by TALON affinity chromatography followed by SEC on the HiLoad 16/600 Superdex 75 column (Cytiva) in 10 mM Tris pH 8.0 and 100 mM NaCl. The sEC1–2 plasmid, which also included a FLAG tag, was used to transfect FreeStyle 293-FS cells (Thermo Fisher Scientific) at a density of 1 × 106 cells per ml using linear PEI (Polysciences). After incubation for 6 days at 37 °C, the supernatant was collected and clarified by centrifugation at 4,000 rpm for 20 min. Proteins were purified by nickel-chelation chromatography with Nickel-NTA resin (Qiagen). The protein was eluted in PBS containing 250 mM imidazole, and the buffer was exchanged with PBS.

To obtain full-length IgG versions of the monoclonal antibodies used in this study, synthetic variable regions of the light and heavy chains for each antibody were cloned into pCAGGS plasmids upstream of the corresponding human IgG1 constant regions. All antibodies were expressed in Expi293 cells as described above. Antibodies were purified by protein G affinity chromatography, eluted with 0.1 M glycine pH 2.7, neutralized with 1 M Tris-HCl pH 8.0 and further purified by SEC on the HiLoad 16/600 Superdex 200 column (Cytiva) in PBS. To obtain scFv versions of the monoclonal antibodies used in this study, we cloned the variable regions of the heavy and light chains joined by a linker (GTGGSGGGGSGGGGSGGGAS) into a pMT/BiP plasmid in frame with an enterokinase cleavage site and a double-Strep tag. These plasmids were used to generate stable transfectants of Drosophila S2 cells, as described above. Antibody fragments were purified by Strep-Tactin affinity chromatography using the StrepTrap HP column (Cytiva), followed by SEC on a HiLoad 16/600 Superdex 75 column (Cytiva) in PBS.

To perform the cryo-EM studies, we diluted the purified soluble spikes alone or in a complex with PCDH1 EC1–4 to a concentration of 0.34 mg ml−1 in 10 mM Tris pH 8.0, 100 mM NaCl and deposited 3 μl on UltrAufoil R1.2/1.3 300 mesh copper grids that had been glow-discharged twice for 25 s in the PELCO easyglow cleaning system (15 mA, 0.3 mBar). After 5 s of wait time, excess protein was blotted away for 3 s using Whatman No.1 filter paper with a blot force of −1 at 15 °C in 100% humidity before being plunge frozen in liquid ethane using Vitrobot Mark IV (Thermo Fisher Scientific). Frozen grids were imaged on the Titan Krios POLARIS (Synchrotron Soleil) system equipped with a FALCON 4i detector (Thermo Fisher Scientific), operating in super-resolution mode at a magnification of ×165,000, corresponding to a calibrated pixel size of 0.7455 Å per pixel. A full description of the cryo-EM data-collection parameters is provided in Supplementary Table 1.

For the VLPs, the R1.2/R1.3 300 mesh ultrAuFoil grids were glow-discharged with a setting of 20 mA, 1 min. Then, 3 μl concentrated PCDH1-EC1–GS5–ANDV-DM-VLP sample was added onto glow-discharged grids, blotted with a blot force of 4 and a blot time of 20 s and plunge-frozen into liquid ethane using a Vitrobot Mark IV (Thermo Fisher Scientific). The prepared grids were screened for optimal ice thickness on a 200 kV Glacios (Thermo Fisher Scientific) at The University of Texas at Austin. Screened grids were transferred to the cryo-EM facilities at UT Southwestern Medical Center (UTSW) for high-resolution data collection. Data collection was performed on the Titan Krios G4 (Thermo Fisher Scientific) system equipped with an X-FEG, K3 Summit camera (Gatan) and BioContinuum Imaging Filter (Gatan). A total of 18,507 videos was acquired at a pixel size of 0.413 Å, with a total dose of 56 e− Å−2 and a 15 eV energy filter slit width.

Motion correction, CTF estimation, particle picking, 2D classification, 3D classification and non-uniform 3D refinement were performed using cryoSPARC (v.4.6.2)48. For model building, the map was sharpened using DeepEMhancer49. The initial model was generated using ChimeraX50 by fitting the high-resolution ANDV tetramer structure (PDB: 9P3I)5 and the crystallographic structure of PCDH1 EC1 (PDB: 6BX7)26 in the electron density map. The model was further built manually in Coot51 and refined iteratively using phenix.refine52 using a refinement strategy that includes minimization_global, local_grid_search and adp, with rotamer and Ramachandran restraints. The full cryo-EM data processing workflow is shown in Supplementary Fig. 1 and the model refinement statistics are provided in Supplementary Table 1.

For the VLPs, the data processing follows a similar pipeline reported for ANDV glycoprotein single tetramer5. In brief, the dataset was imported into CryoSPARC (v.4.6.2)48 for patch-based motion correction, contrast transfer function (CTF) estimation and exposure curation. A model manually trained on 200 motion-corrected, dose-weighted micrographs was then used to predict particle coordinates within crYOLO (v.1.9.9)53. Picked particles were then imported into CryoSPARC for particle extraction and 2D classification. The ANDV glycoprotein tetramer EM map was used as the reference for the homogeneous refinement to get the initial model. Then, recentring particles and particle re-extraction were performed, followed by rounds of homogeneous refinement with C4 symmetry, 3D classification, reference-based motion correction and non-uniform refinement. In the final round of 3D classification, to extract potentially different binding poses of PCDH1–EC1 to the ANDV tetramer, the particles were first symmetry expanded, and a 3D classification was performed on the expanded particle datasets with a sphere mask created in ChimeraX centred on the PCDH1 EC1 density. The classified particles were then locally refined, and the refined EM maps were sharpened by EMReady (v.2.0)54 to discriminate different binding poses.

ELISA plates (Thermo Fisher Scientific, 439454) were coated with 100 µl of recombinant His tagged PCDH1 EC1–4 (WT or double mutant) diluted to 10 µg ml−1 in PBS (pH 7.4). After overnight incubation at 4 °C, plates were washed three times with PBS containing 0.05% Tween-20 (PBS-T) and blocked with 250 µl per well of blocking buffer (PBS supplemented with 5% non-fat dry milk and 0.05% Tween-20). For the binding experiment with ANDV (Gn–Gc)4 spikes (Fig. 1b), serial dilutions of the spike were prepared in blocking buffer and 100 µl per well were added to the wells and incubated for 4 h at room temperature with agitation at 300 rpm. The plates were then washed as described above, and bound spike protein was detected by adding 100 µl per well of anti-Strep-tag monoclonal antibody (C23.21, Thermo Fisher Scientific, 910STRL) and incubating for 90 min at room temperature with agitation at 300 rpm. After washing, 100 µl per well of anti-mouse HRP-conjugated antibody (Jackson ImmunoResearch, 115-035-146, 1:10,000) was added and incubated for 60 min at room temperature. The plates were washed five times, and binding was detected using TMB substrate (ELISA 1-Step Ultra TMB, Thermo Fisher Scientific, 34028). The reaction was stopped with 50 µl per well of 2 M H2SO4, and the absorbance at 450 nm was measured using the Tecan Infinite M100 multimode microplate reader. Reported values correspond to the mean and s.d. from independent experiments, each performed in triplicate.

For the competition experiments with soluble sEC1 WT or the double mutant (Fig. 1c) or with human IgGs or scFvs (Fig. 4b), purified protein or antibodies were either diluted to final concentrations of 10, 2, 0.4 and 0.08 µM in blocking buffer supplemented with 500 nM ANDV tetrameric spike protein or serially diluted (starting at 1.2 µM) and supplemented with 0.36 µl of FSL-biotin labelled rVSV-ANDV per dilution. After pre-incubation for 1 h at room temperature, the spike- or virus-competitor mixtures were added to the wells of WT PCDH1 EC1–4- or EC1–2-coated plates and the experiment proceeded as described in the previous paragraph.

For rVSV chimera ELISAs, 96-well ELISA plates (Corning) were coated with purified sEC1–2 (100 ng per well) overnight at 4 °C, washed briefly with PBS and blocked with 5% non-fat dry milk in PBS (2 h at room temperature). rVSV chimeras were normalized based on Gc expression by western blotting (see specifics below) and membrane-labelled with a short-chain phospholipid probe, functional-component spacer diacyl lipid conjugated to biotin (FSL-biotin (5 µg ml−1); Sigma-Aldrich) for 1 h at 37 °C. The rVSVs were then serial diluted twofold and added to sEC1–2-coated wells (1 h at 37 °C). Bound rVSVs were detected by incubation with Pierce high-sensitivity Streptavidin–horseradish peroxidase (HRP) conjugate (1:10,000 dilution, Thermo Fisher Scientific). The ELISA signal was developed using 1-Step Ultra TMB-ELISA substrate solution (Thermo Fisher Scientific) and measured at an absorbance at 450 nm on the Cytation5 cell imaging multi-mode reader (Agilent BioTek Gen5 Microplate Reader and Imager software, v.3.2). Exact P values for Fig. 3b were as follows: ANDV versus SNV: 4.7 × 10−13, ANDV versus ANDVSNV-all: 0.62, ANDV versus ANDVSNV-CDLoop: 9.1 × 10−5 (left); SNV versus ANDV: 4.7 × 10−13, SNV versus SNVANDV-all: 4.7 × 10−13, SNV versus SNVANDV-CDLoop: 4.7 × 10−13 (middle); and HTNV versus ANDV: 1.1 × 10−12, HTNV versus HTNVANDV-all: 8.9 × 10−7, HTNV versus HTNVANDV-CDLoop: 0.99 (right).

BLI data shown in this Article were produced using the Octet R8 (Sartorius). The BLI data shown in Fig. 1d were generated as follows. First, Ni-NTA sensors (Sartorius) were activated in 10 mM NiCl2 and then dipped into a solution of His-tagged PCDH1 EC1–4 at 2 μM in PBS pH 7.4 for 600 s. Next, sensors were quenched in solutions of PBS pH 7.4 with 500 nM His-tagged human TNF and 1 mg ml−1 BSA for 600 s. The functionalized sensors were then dipped into solution containing soluble spikes diluted at different concentrations (900, 300, 112, 100 and 33 nM) in PBS pH 7.4 supplemented with 1 mg ml−1 BSA (PBS-BSA) and finally moved to PBS-BSA for dissociation. Association and dissociation rates were recorded for 300 s. Non-specific interactions were monitored using a sensor coated with unrelated His-tagged protein (TNF) and signals were subtracted. The curves were processed using the Octet Analysis Studio software (v.13.0; Sartorius), and the KD values were obtained after fitting to a 1:1 global model.

The BLI data shown in Extended Data Fig. 1b were generated as follows. First, Strep sensors (Sartorius, 18-0063) were dipped into a solution of strep-tagged (Gn–Gc)4 ANDV soluble spike at 20 nM in PBS pH 7.4 for 2,400 s. Next, sensors were quenched in solutions of PBS pH 7.4 with 100 nM strep-tagged Vaccinia virus D8 protein and 1 mg ml−1 BSA for 1,200 s. The functionalized biosensors were then dipped into solutions containing soluble PCDH1 EC1–4 at different concentrations (2,000, 1,000, 500 and 250 nM) in PBS pH 7.4 supplemented with 1 mg ml−1 BSA and finally moved to PBS-BSA for dissociation. Association and dissociation rates were recorded for 3,600 s, and non-specific interactions were monitored using a sensor coated with unrelated strep-tagged vaccinia D8 protein.

For crystallization of the complex GnH–scFv-MIB22, we produced GnH (amino acids 21–374) of ANDV and the scFv of MIB22 in D. melanogaster S2 cells, as described above. We removed the purification tags using enterokinase before running SEC to isolate the complex, which was concentrated in 10 mM Tris-HCl pH 8.0 and 150 mM NaCl using Vivaspin centricons. Crystallization screening trials were performed using the vapour-diffusion method on the Mosquito nanodispensing system (STPLabtech) according to established protocols55. Crystals were grown in 0.2 M CaCl2, 0.1 M Tris-HCl pH 8.5, 20% (w/v) PEG 4000 and were cryo-protected with 20% (v/v) glycerol, followed by rapid transfer into liquid nitrogen. Diffraction images were integrated with XDS (v.10 January 2022)56, and crystallography calculations were carried out with programs from the CCP4 program suite (v.9)57. The structure was solved by molecular replacement with Phaser (v.2.8.2) from the suite PHENIX (v.1.19.2-4158)58 using PUUV GnH (PDB: 5FXU) and the scFv C10 of dengue virus (PDB: 4UT9) as search models. The final model was built by combining real space model building in Coot (v.0.9.8.92)51 with reciprocal space refinement with phenix.refine (v.1.14rc3). The final model was validated with Molprobity59. The crystallography statistics and PDB codes are provided in Supplementary Table 1. The structural analysis software was installed using SBGRID60.

The development of the SHV system, a Semliki Forest virus (SFV) replicon expressing an adaptively mutated ANDV Gn–Gc (Chilean field strain R123, GenBank: NP_604472.1) in place of its structural proteins in the pCMV-SFV4 plasmid61 to study hantavirus entry, will be described elsewhere (L.K. et al., manuscript submitted). Transfection of a single (pSHV-ANDV) plasmid encoding the SHV genome into Huh7.5.1 cells produces self-replicating viruses, enabling the genotype–phenotype link required for deep mutagenesis scanning of Gn–Gc proteins. To generate SHV Gn mutants encompassing amino acids 24–471 of its ectodomain, the Gn sequence was divided into six sections based on the availability of unique restriction sites: lib1 (amino acids 24–92), lib2 (amino acids 101–166), lib3 (amino acids 170–230), lib4 (amino acids 235–312), lib5 (amino acids 316–386) and lib6 (amino acids 390–471). The lib1 sequence was flanked by NruI (5′ end) and AclI (3′ end); lib2 by AclI (5′ end) and PspXI (3′ end); lib3 by PspXI (5′ end) and XmnI (3′ end); lib4 by XmnI (5′ end) and BbvCI (3′ end); lib5 by BbvCI (5′ end) and XmaI (3′ end); and lib6 by XmaI (5′ end) and BlpI (3′ end). Site-saturation variant libraries of oligo pools carrying single amino acid changes, with one codon per amino acid for all 20 amino acids, including the parental sequence, were synthesized by Twist Biosciences and cloned into the pSHV vector by digesting the vector at the corresponding restriction sites and assembling the fragments using NEB HiFi DNA assembly. The parental Gn sequence was also included in each library. The selected codons for each amino acid were optimal for human cell expression: Ala, GCC; Cys, TGC; Asp, GAC; Glu, GAG; Phe, TTC; Gly, GGC; His, CAC; Ile, ATC; Lys, AAG; Leu, CTG; Met, ATG; Asn, AAC; Pro, CCC; Gln, CAG; Arg, CGC; Ser, AGC; Thr, ACC; Val, GTG; Trp, TGG; and Tyr, TAC. The sequences of the assembled plasmid libraries were verified by Illumina MiSeq amplicon sequencing (see below for details).

Individual pSHV-ANDV sublibrary (lib1–6) plasmid pools were transfected using Lipofectamine-3000 according to the manufacturer’s instructions, at 10 μg per 100 mm plate of Huh7.5.1 cells, and SHVs were collected at 72 h after transfection to generate passage 0 stocks. To eliminate trans-complementation of dysfunctional Gn–Gc variants by parental SHV or other functional variants, the library was passaged twice at a multiplicity of infection (MOI) of 0.1 in Huh7.5.1 cells (a gift from C. M. Rice62 and F. V. Chisari63). Viral supernatants collected at passage 2 were clarified by centrifugation at 4,500 rpm at 4 °C for 15 min, repeated twice. Viruses in the clarified supernatant were concentrated by ultracentrifugation at 25,000 rpm at 4 °C for 1:45 h. The virus pellet was resuspended in 1× serum-free DMEM and incubated overnight at 4 °C. The resuspended pellet was then overlaid on a 10% sucrose cushion and ultracentrifuged at 25,000 rpm at 4 °C for 1:45 h. Subsequently, viral pellets were resuspended in 1× NT Buffer (10 mM Tris-Cl, 135 mM NaCl, pH 7.5) and stored at −80 °C before further downstream analysis. The SHV titres were estimated by immunostaining naive Huh7.5.1 cells infected with limiting dilutions of SHVs for expression of SFV non-structural protein 1 (nsP1) at 12 h after infection, using rabbit antisera (1:300) provided by A. Merits at the University of Tartu64, followed by an Alexa-Fluor-488-conjugated anti-rabbit secondary antibody (1:500).

To enrich PCDH1-binding SHV particles, a sEC1–2 capture ELISA was performed as described previously1,18. First, the amounts of SHV particles across the sublibraries and parental SHVs were normalized by labelling the pelleted SHV particle membranes with FSL-biotin (functional spacer di-acyl lipid probe conjugated to biotin) (Sigma-Aldrich). Equal amounts of FSL-biotin (10 μg ml−1) and pelleted SHVs were mixed and incubated at 37 °C for 1 h for labelling. Serial twofold dilutions of labelled SHVs were coated on a high-binding ELISA plate (Corning) at 37 °C for 1 h. After removing unbound virus and washing with 1× PBS three times for 5 min each, the wells were blocked with 5% non-fat dry milk (Bio-Rad) prepared in 1× PBS for 1 h at 37 °C. The FSL-labelled SHVs were detected using a high-sensitivity streptavidin-HRP conjugated antibody (BioLegend) at a final dilution of 1:3,000 after incubation at 37 °C for 1 h. The ELISA signal was developed using Ultra-TMB ELISA substrate (Thermo Fisher Scientific) and measured at 450 nm on the Cytation-5 multi-mode plate reader (BioTek, Agilent). SHV input amounts for the sEC1–2 capture ELISA for each sublibrary were calculated from half-maximum effective concentration values that were obtained by fitting the data to a sigmoidal four-parameter logistic model, with the logarithm of the virus amount on the x axis, using Prism GraphPad v.11.

For the sEC1–2 capture ELISA, high-affinity 96-well ELISA plates (Corning) were coated with sEC1-2 (100 ng per well) at 4 °C overnight. The next day, after a single wash with 1× PBS, the plates were blocked with 5% blotting-grade non-fat dry milk (Bio-Rad) in 1× PBS for 1 h at 37 °C. Normalized amounts of pelleted parental and sublibrary SHVs labelled with FSL-biotin, as described above, were applied to the sEC1–2-coated wells for 1 h at 37 °C. After three thorough washes with 1× PBS to remove unbound SHVs, the bound viruses were processed for RNA isolation and PCR with reverse transcription (RT–PCR) as described below. Based on infectious units, sufficient SHV particles were included to ensure 1,000× coverage in the input of each sublibrary for the capture ELISA, and 10–12 wells per sublibrary were pooled for RNA isolation. For each capture ELISA, a parallel set of wells was processed for streptavidin–HRP-based detection of bound SHV particles, as described above for the normalization ELISA.

PCDH1-bound SHVs from independent biological duplicates for each sublibrary were processed by adding 50 μl of Zymogen RNA lysis buffer per well, incubating at room temperature for 8 min, and then isolating RNA using the Quick RNA Viral Kit according to the manufacturer’s instructions (Zymo Research). Input SHVs for each sublibrary were also sequenced to establish the baseline. cDNA was synthesized using the SuperScript III First-Strand Synthesis System (Invitrogen) according to the manufacturer’s instructions, with a reverse primer (5′-ATTGCCTACCCTAACACTAGTTGCAG-3′) located downstream of the Gn–Gc sequence. Sufficient viral RNA was used to ensure 1,000× coverage of each sublibrary. cDNA synthesis was followed by PCR with ANDV Gn-specific primers tagged at their 5′-ends with Illumina MiSeq adapters for the forward (5′-TCGTCGGCAGCGTCAGATGTGTATAAGAGACAG-3′) and reverse (5′-GTCTCGTGGGCTCGGAGATGTGTATAAGAGACAG-3′) sequences for each sublibrary. The sequences of the Gn-specific part of these primers were as follows: lib1-F, 5′-GTGCTATACTCTGACTCTCGCGATG-3′ and lib1-R, 5′- CGGTCTTTGTCTGTGCCTCAAAC-3′; lib2-F, 5′-CCACCAACGCAGCTTCCAC-3′ and lib2-R 5′-TTCTCATATATCACCTGTATCCTGTATCCTCGAGGT-3′; lib3-F, 5′-CTTGCATGGCCTCTGTGTTCAC-3′ and lib3-R, 5′-TGTGCACCCTGTCTTGGTGA-3′; lib4-F, 5′-AGCTGAAGGTGATCAAAACATTTGAAGG-3′ and lib4-R 5′-ACCCCGGCGAAAGCTGTC-3′; lib5-F, 5′-GTTCCTTCAACCTCTTCTACAGATACGC-3′ and lib5-R, 5′-TGTAGGCCTCGCAAGAAGCA-3′; and lib6-F, 5′-TGTTCTGTACTCTGGCCGGC-3′ and lib6-R, 5′-AGGGACACAGTTCGACGG-3′. Cycling conditions for the PCR were initial denaturation at 98 °C for 1 min; followed by 30 cycles of denaturation at 98 °C for 15 s, annealing at 65 °C for 30 s, extension at 72 °C for 30 s; and then a final extension step at 72 °C for 5 min. After verification of amplified PCR products on a 1% agarose gel, the products were purified using Ampure XP beads and indexed by a second round of PCR, followed by another cleanup with Ampure XP beads. Completed libraries were assessed by gel electrophoresis using the automated Agilent TapeStation D1000 Kit (Agilent Technologies) and quantified using the Qubit dsDNA BR Assay Kit (Thermo Fisher Scientific). Libraries were normalized to 40 nM, pooled and diluted to 4 nM. The 4 nM pool was denatured and diluted to 12.5 pM, then spiked with 10% Phi X DNA to increase base diversity. Paired-end 2 × 250 sequencing was performed on the Illumina MiSeq system using the Illumina MiSeq Reagent v2 kit (Illumina). Primary data analysis, base calling and quality scoring were performed using the onboard MiSeq Control Software v.4.1.0.480 and RTA v.1.18.54.4. Samples were demultiplexed, adapter sequences were trimmed and FASTQ files were generated for downstream analysis.

The FASTQ files were aligned to the parental reference sequence using the Burrows–Wheeler Alignment Tool (BWA, v.0.7.17). Sequencing reads were processed using a custom Python-based DMS pipeline. Paired-end reads were first filtered to retain only high-quality sequences that (1) matched expected primer regions within defined mismatches; (2) had limited ambiguous base calls (N); and (3) showed sufficient overlap between R1 and R2 reads. Trimmed reads were merged, and coding regions were translated to protein sequences using a codon dictionary described above. To identify valid single amino acid variants, translated protein sequences were compared to the parental sequence. Only sequences differing by one amino acid and free of stop codons were retained. For each variant, the mutation position (adjusted by an offset to match full-length protein numbering) and substituted residue were recorded. Mutation counts were aggregated per position and normalized to compute the relative frequency of each variant, generating a position-wise matrix of single amino acid substitutions. These results were saved in a CSV file and used for downstream analysis.

For each set (input and bound replicates), CSVs containing Mut_type, Mutation_count and Position_count were parsed to extract a numeric position from the leading digits of Mut_type (for example, 24C → 24) and define Variant = Mut_type. Variant-level enrichment in each bound replicate versus input was quantified using a smoothed within-position frequency with pseudocount pc = 0.5: freq = (mutation_count + pc)/(position_count + 2 pc), and enrichment was reported as log2[FC]= log2[freq_bound/freq_input]. For each variant, a two-sided Fisher’s exact test compared the variant versus all other reads at that position between bound and input using a 2 × 2 table (mut, nonmut=position_count−mutation_count), followed by Benjamini–Hochberg FDR correction per replicate. Reproducibility across bound replicates was assessed by Pearson/Spearman correlation of log2[FC] values and by calling variants reproducibly enriched/depleted if they had the same sign of log2[FC] in both replicates and FDR < 0.05 in both. Finally, an input-only filter was applied: only variants with input mutation_count ≥ 10, an arbitrary limit, were retained for the filtered master workbook (bound counts were not filtered). A custom Python code used to process the raw FASTQ files to variant counts per position is available at GitHub (https://github.com/Jwang30/DMS_Calling).

Replication-competent rVSVs expressing an eGFP reporter and bearing hantavirus Gn–Gc of ANDV (NP_604472.1), SNV (NP_941974.1) or HTNV (NP_941978.1), with previously described modifications22, or chimeric variants of the three listed, were generated using a plasmid-based rescue system in HEK293T cells (ATCC, authenticated by short-tandem-repeat profiling) and propagated on Vero (ATCC, authenticated by karyotyping) or HUH7.5.1 (J. Carette, not authenticated) cells as described previously65,66. The constructs encoding the chimeric Gn–Gcs were cloned into the pVSV plasmid using standard molecular techniques. The chimera rVSVs contained residue swaps between ANDV and SNV, and ANDV and HTNV within the receptor-binding site, encompassing the three EC1-interacting loops (AB, CD and DE) or the CD-loop alone. Additional mutations were included to accommodate space for the loop swaps between the NWHs and OWHs. The comprehensive list of Gn amino acid changes in the ‘CD loop’ and ‘all’ chimeras is as follows (using ANDV numbering). CD Loop: ANDV residues 118–127 and adjacent residues 40 and 251. All: residues 35–38, 40, 118–127, 153, 156–157, 180, 195, 251, 257 and 413–419. A visual summary of all of the swaps within Gn is provided in Extended Data Fig. 4. After viral plaque purification, the sequences of all rVSV glycoproteins were amplified from viral genomic RNA by RT–PCR and analysed using Sanger sequencing.

To evaluate the ability of PCDH1 to block infection of the rVSV chimeras, rVSVs bearing either WT or swapped PCDH1-interacting loops (all three loops: AB, CD, DE or CD alone) of ANDV/SNV and ANDV/HTNV were diluted to an MOI of 0.1 and incubated with threefold serial dilutions of soluble PCDH1 EC1–2 (WT or mutant containing the substitutions F83A and D85R) at room temperature for 1 h before being added to HUVEC (Thermo Fisher Scientific, authenticated by Invitrogen by cell morphology and cell-type-specific markers) monolayers in 96-well plates. rVSVs bearing ANDV Gn–Gc with HTNV-all residue swap did not readily infect HUVECs and therefore could not be evaluated. Exact P values for Fig. 3c (sEC1–2WT) were as follows: ANDV versus SNV: 1.0 × 10−15, ANDV versus ANDVSNV-all: 1.0 × 10−15, ANDV versus ANDVSNV-CDLoop: 1.6 × 10−7 (left); SNV versus ANDV: 1.0 × 10−15, SNV versus SNVANDV-all: 6.1 × 10−5, SNV versus SNVANDV-CDLoop: 8.9 × 10−3 (middle); and HTNV versus ANDV: 1.0 × 10−15, HTNV versus HTNVANDV-all: 5.4 × 10−9, HTNV versus HTNVANDV-CDLoop: 0.99 (right). Exact P values for Fig. 3c, bottom row (sEC1-2MUT) were as follows: ANDV versus SNV: 1.00, ANDV versus ANDVSNV-all: 0.69, ANDV versus ANDVSNV-CDLoop: 1.00 (left); SNV versus ANDV: 1.00, SNV versus SNVANDV-all: 0.68, SNV versus SNVANDV-CDLoop: 1.00 (middle); and HTNV versus ANDV: 0.02, HTNV versus HTNVANDV-all: 0.37, HTNV versus HTNVANDV-CDLoop: 0.71 (right). To compare the neutralizing activity of four full-length antibodies (ADI-65534, ANDV-34, ANDV-5, and MIB22), rVSV-ANDV (MOI 0.1) was incubated with threefold serial dilutions of each antibody, starting at 33 nM, for 1 h before being added to HUVECs cultured in 96-well plates. For all infections, cells were fixed 14–16 h after infection with 4% formaldehyde (Sigma-Aldrich) and counterstained with Hoechst nuclear stain (Invitrogen). The number of infected cells (GFP-positive cell counts) and viral infectivity (the percentage of GFP-positive cells) were quantified by automated enumeration of eGFP-expressing cells from images acquired using the Cytation 5 Cell Imaging Multi-Mode Reader with Agilent BioTek Gen5 Microplate Reader and Imager software (BioTek, v.3.2).

To normalize the amount of each virus used for rVSV–sEC1–2 ELISAs, the level of glycoprotein (Gc) incorporation was quantified by immunoblotting. rVSV particles bearing ANDV or SNV Gc were detected using an anti-hantavirus Gc rabbit pAb 16281 made from immunized rabbits with the peptide containing ANDV Gc amino acids 1067–1087 (Pacific Immunology). The rVSVs bearing ANDV or HTNV Gc were detected using a mouse anti-hantavirus Gc clone 2H4/F6 monoclonal antibody67. Both antibodies were diluted in 1% NFDM and blots were stained for 1 h at room temperature, followed by washing with PBS with 0.01% Tween-20 (PBST), and incubation with an anti-rabbit IgG Alexa Fluor Plus 680 pAb (Thermo Fisher Scientific), an anti-mouse IgG Alexa Fluor 680 pAb (Thermo Fisher Scientific) or an anti-mouse IgG IRDye-800CW pAb (LI-COR) for 1 h at room temperature. After thoroughly washing with PBST, blots were imaged on the iBright FL1500 Imaging System and analysed using the iBright Analysis Software. The chimera rVSVs were normalized to their WT parent based on the amount of incorporated glycoprotein. Uncropped and unprocessed scans of the blots are provided in Supplementary Fig. 1.

To evaluate the binding of sEC1 to ANDV glycoproteins displayed within a membrane, Vero cells seeded in six-well plates were infected with rVSVs expressing eGFP, bearing either ANDV or HTNV Gn–Gc with an MOI of 0.3 IUs per cell for 1 h. Infection was stopped by the addition of NH4Cl (20 mM). At 14 h after infection, cells were collected with a cell scraper and blocked with chilled 10% FBS in PBS for 30 min at 4 °C. Cells were then incubated for 1 h at 4 °C with soluble PCDH1 EC1–2 (500 nM or 1000 nM) or buffer only (10% FBS in PBS) as a negative control. After multiple washes with PBS, cells were fixed with 4% formaldehyde (Sigma-Aldrich) for 5 min at 4 °C and washed once with PBS. To detect bound sEC1–2, cells were incubated with a mouse anti-DDK(FLAG)-DyLight-594 monoclonal antibody (Thermo Fisher Scientific) for 1 h at 4 °C, followed by multiple washes with PBS. Cells were then resuspended in 2% FBS in PBS, passed through a 41 µm nylon net filter (Millipore) and analysed using the LSR II flow cytometer and FlowJo v.10 software. The gating strategy is provided in Supplementary Fig. 2.

Molecular representations were prepared using PyMol v.3.0.3 (Schrödinger) and ChimeraX (v.1.6.1)50. The panels in Fig. 1a were generated using the web server IBS (v.2.0)68 and the Extended Data Fig. 4a using the server ESPRIPT69,70,71,72. Extended Data Fig. 6a was generated using Seaborn (v.0.13.2)73. Graphs were generated using GraphPad Prism (v.11). Figures were assembled and edited using Adobe Illustrator (v.28.5).

Further information on research design is available in the Nature Portfolio Reporting Summary linked to this article.

Atomic coordinates of the reported structures have been deposited in the PDB under accession codes 30XO and 30XT, and in the EMDB under accession code EMD-58134 and EMDB-77314. The atomic coordinates of PCDH1, ANDV-34, ADI-65534, ANDV-5, the spike protein in eVLPs, the Gn head domain of PUUV and HALC4_136 used in this study are published in the PDB under accession codes 6BX7, 8DBZ, 6P3Y, 9P3I, 5FX4 and 8D09. Raw Illumina sequencing data (FASTQ files) have been deposited in the NCBI Sequence Read Archive (SRA) under BioProject PRJNA1501900. Processed variant-level and site-level score matrices are provided in Supplementary Table 1. A companion document (Supplementary Note 1) describes the workbook contents, data fields and associated analyses. Source data are provided with this paper.

A custom Python code used to process the raw FASTQ files to variant counts per position is available at GitHub (https://github.com/Jwang30/DMS_Calling) and has been archived at Zenodo74 (https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22751343).

Gn (pink) and Gc (light yellow) ectodomains are shown as surfaces, with one heterodimer highlighted as cartoon and in darker colours. Flowchart illustrating the data processing steps used to obtain the high-resolution structure of the spike/receptor complex. Particle counts and filters used at each step are indicated. Movies were collected on a Titan Krios (POLARIS, Synchrotron Soleil) equipped with a FALCON 4i detector (Thermo Fisher Scientific) operating at a calibrated pixel size of 0.7455 Å/px. After motion correction and CTF estimation, particles were picked sequentially by blob picking and template picking and extracted with a 52 px box size at 5.964 Å/px. Junk particles were removed by iterative runs of 2D classification, and the final set was reextracted with a 416 px box size at 0.7455 Å/px. An initial map was obtained after a one-class Ab-initio reconstruction followed by a Non-Uniform refinement using C4 symmetry. To improve the quality of the central density across the fourfold symmetry axis, a 3D classification (5 classes, 6 Å filter) was performed using the focus mask shown as a transparent magenta surface in the figure. All the particles with an additional density in the centre of the spike were selected and the final map was obtained after a Non-Uniform refinement with C4 symmetry using the marginalization symmetry relaxation method. For model building the map was sharpened using DeepEMhancer. All the workflow was performed using cryoSPARC v4.6.2.

(A) Map/model correlation coefficients for the four Gn chains (left panel), the four Gc chains (right panel), and the EC1 domain of PCDH1 (bottom-left panel). Gc domains are indicated above the panel to show that the lowest correlation coefficients are concentrated in domain III and the N-terminal tail. The bottom-right panel shows two views of the ANDV spike coloured according to the correlation coefficient, as indicated. (B) The four Gn protomers in the soluble spike were aligned and coloured by RMSD (left panel). PCDH1 EC1 bound to GnA is shown in blue to highlight the main interaction with Gn. The right panels show the quality of the cryo-EM map for the loops CD and DE of the four Gn chains. Gn is coloured red, PCDH1 blue and the maps are displayed at an absolute contour level of 0.1. The cryo-EM densities for these loops in chain A, which establishes most of the contacts with the receptor, are much clearer than those in chains C, E, and G. (C) Structural alignment of the four Gn/Gc heterodimers in the soluble spike to the reported Gn/Gc structure obtained in VLPs (PDB 9P3I). In the left panel Gn is coloured in pink and Gc in yellow, and in the right panel the structure is coloured by RMSD. (D) Slight rotation of the soluble ANDV spike. The soluble spike obtained in this study was structurally aligned to the reported ANDV spike in VLPs (PDB 9P3I) in the Gn Base domain. A 2.9° rotation was measured between the centre of mass of Gn and the C alpha atom of Asp91 in both tetramers.

#2129. ICE arrests attorney after alleged threats to federal agents

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

Lisa White, 70, of Diamondhead in Long Beach, Mississippi, was arresed last month after she allegedly threatened to kill federal agents while trying to buy a gun and pepper spray. (Photo from U.S. Immigration and Customs Enforcement’s Oct. 21 news release)

An attorney from Long Beach, Mississippi, was arrested last month by U.S. Immigration and Customs Enforcement’s Homeland Security Investigations after she allegedly threatened to kill federal agents while trying to buy a gun and pepper spray.

Lisa White, 70, of Diamondhead in Long Beach, Mississippi, is licensed in Florida. She is being held as agents prepare federal criminal charges for influencing, impeding or retaliating against a federal official by threat.

White was arrested by special agents Sept. 24. Her trial is set for Nov. 16. She faces a maximum of 10 years in prison, a fine up to $250,000, up to three years supervised release and a $100 special assessment.

#2121. Florida Bar fires employee after investigation into abusive messages to football player

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Damon Wilson II of the Miami Hurricanes rushes the line during the second half of the game against the Wake Forest Demon Deacons on Sept. 18 in Winston-Salem, North Carolina. (Photo by Jared C. Tilton/Getty Images)

The Florida Bar has dismissed a computer technician after a short investigation into abusive Instagram messages sent to a defensive lineman for the University of Miami.

According to a story by the Associated Press, the Florida Bar, which regulates attorneys in the state, said it terminated Kade McLendon on Wednesday. McLendon had been with the Florida Bar for roughly two years.

The AP reported that Damon Wilson II received messages over Instagram that contained multiple racist and homophobic slurs after the Miami Hurricanes’ 43-35 victory over the Florida State Seminoles, its in-state rival team. According to the AP, the messages came from an account from a person using McLendon’s name.

In a statement Wednesday, the Florida Bar told the AP that it condemned the messages as reprehensible and is at odds with its mission, values and policies. McLendon denied sending them, the Florida Bar said, but he did not offer proof strong enough to convince it that his account had been compromised.

The AP said it tried to reach McLendon on Tuesday without success.
